Title: Noncommutative Cluster Varieties and Moduli Spaces of Local Systems

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.27284v1

Published Time: Fri, 28 Aug 2026 01:29:15 GMT

Number of Pages: 133

Markdown Content:
NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 

ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Abstract. In this article, we construct noncommutative cluster varieties, AR,S , for each reduced root system R and marked surface S simultaneously generalizing the cluster varieties of Fock-Goncharov, Li, Goncharov-Shen, Berenstein-Retakh, Goncharov-Kontsevich, and our previously introduced polygonal cluster algebras. Additionally, we define a large class of algebraic groups, we call Jordan split groups. Given a reduced root system R and a family of Jordan algebras, the Lie algebra for G is constructed by unifying the Tits-Kantor-Koecher construction for a single Jordan algebra with the construction of a split Lie algebra. The notion of Jordan split groups is closely related to a grading of its Lie algebra by the root system R. We show that these gradings are usually induced by a choice of standard parabolic subalgebra pΘ and we classify R-graded pairs (G, Θ) via a condition depending only on the subset Θ ⊂ ∆ of the set of simple roots, providing many groups to which our results apply. Jordan split groups can be pinned using Jordan algebras, similarly to the pinnings of classically split groups. This allows us to develop a corresponding root system calculus. Using this, we compare the Weyl groups of R and G, study properties of the PΘ-Bruhat decomposition, give a Lusztig style parameterization of the unipotent subgroup UΘ based on words in the Weyl group W (R), study decomposition relations between group elements, and define the noncommutative cluster varieties AR,S .We define Jordan algebra points of AR,S which parameterize G-local systems on S with boundary decoration related to cosets G/U Θ when G is Jordan split of type R. The cluster modular group ΓR,S considered by Goncharov-Shen, which in particular contains the mapping class group of S, acts on this noncommutative cluster variety by noncommutative quasi-cluster automorphisms. When S is a disk, points of AR,S parameterize configurations of decorated flags. We use this to give noncommutative cluster structures on the double R-Bruhat cells of 

G, generalizing the cluster algebras of Berenstein-Fomin-Zelevinsky. When each Jordan algebra is formally real, we say that G has a positive structure with respect to Θ. We define positive semigroups U >0Θ ⊂ UΘ and G>0Θ ⊂ G. For real algebraic groups, the pairs (G, Θ) which have positive structures are exactly those which admit a positive structure as defined by Guichard-Wienhard. As a consequence, our cluster structure gives explicit positivity tests, and can be used to give algebraic proofs of many of the properties of positive configurations of flags and of positive representations. D.K. was supported by the Danish National Research Foundation (CPH-GEOTOP-DNRF151) and the Alexander von Humboldt Foundation. Z.G., M.N. and A.W. were supported by the European Research Council under ERC-Advanced Grant 101018839. A.W. thanks the Hector Fellow Academy for support. Part of this work was done at the Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton, and at the Erwin Schrödinger International Institute for Mathematics and Physics. We would also like to thank Max Riestenberg, Clarence Kineider, Kaitao Xie, Linhui Shen, and Alexander Goncharov for many helpful discussions.

1. Introduction 

Split real semisimple Lie groups are the simplest among all real semisimple Lie groups. One of their key features is that they can be elegantly assembled from special three dimensional subgroups that arise from the sl (2) -subalgebras associated to a set of positive simple roots. This leads to a characterization of split real Lie groups by their Dynkin diagrams, which in turn are classified by type An, B n, C n, D n, E 6, E 7, E 8, F 4, or G2. The property that split real Lie groups are built up from SL 2(R)’s plays a key role in their structure theory and underlies many interesting developments in recent years. Some examples are (1) Decomposition theorems generalizing the Gauss decomposition of matrices. (2) The structure theory of totally positive semigroups by Lusztig [Lus94], their cluster structure and explicit positivity criteria developed in [BFZ05]. (3) Parameterizations of configuration spaces of flags and applications to cluster coordinates on higher Teichmüller spaces [FG06, GS19]. In this paper, we show that when we allow more general building blocks, many real semisimple Lie groups, and more generally semisimple algebraic groups, can be built up in a similar way. This allows us to draw interesting conclusions on their structure theory and give vast general-izations of the above mentioned applications to a broad family of real semisimple Lie groups. In particular, this includes all real semisimple Lie groups which admit a positive structure relative to a subset of simple roots Θ as introduced in [GW25]. To motivate the basic building blocks we use, let us consider the simplest split real simple Lie group, SL 2(R), which is the building block for split real semisimple groups. SL 2(R) is itself built from more elementary pieces; two abelian additive groups, or “root groups”, glued together along a multiplicative group, or a “torus”. This manifest as the decomposition of a generic element g

in SL 2(R) as:

This is called the Gauss decomposition. The matrices on the left and right sides belong to the root groups, while the matrix in the middle is in the torus. The root groups are isomorphic to the additive group (R, +) , while the torus is the multiplicative group (R×, ·). The torus acts on the root groups by conjugation:

The map ι in fact provides a quadratic action of the root group on itself given by ι(a)( b) = a2b.This structure of SL 2(R) can be generalized in a much broader context, since we used very few properties of R. In particular we did not even use that R is commutative. The key structure is the map ι and the quadratic action it defines of R on itself. There is a well known generalization of this structure called a quadratic Jordan algebra .A quadratic Jordan algebra is given by a triple J = ( V, ι, 1) of a vector space V , a map ι : V →

End( V ) and a special element 1 ∈ V which is sent to the identity. The image of ι generates a subgroup of GL( V ) called the structure group of J denoted by Γ( J). An example is the vector space of k × k matrices over a field K with 1 being the identity matrix and ι given NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 5

by ι(A)( B) = ABA . In this case, the structure group is a subgroup of GL k × GL k. Given a quadratic Jordan algebra J, the Tits-Kantor-Koecher construction creates a Lie algebra sl 2(J),from which we obtain an associated group SL 2(J). This group can be thought of as two copies of the vector space V , replacing the root groups of SL 2, glued along the (noncommutative) structure group, replacing the torus of SL 2.These SL 2(J)’s are the basic building blocks we use. We call the groups which are built from these building blocks in the same way as split groups are built from SL 2’s Jordan split groups .We show that many semisimple algebraic groups can be realized as Jordan split groups, e.g. any semisimple algebraic group with a reduced root system is a Jordan split group. Viewing them this way provides interesting new structure on these groups and allows us to construct generalizations of the above mentioned features. In particular we obtain: (1) Decomposition theorems generalizing the Gauss decomposition. (2) Non-commutative cluster structures on particular double Bruhat cells of G generalizing those of [BFZ05]. (3) A cluster structure and explicit positivity criteria for groups admitting a positive struc-ture with respect to Θ as defined in [GW25]. (4) Parameterizations of configuration spaces of flags via non-commutative cluster struc-tures, generalizing at the same time [FG06, Le19, GS19] as well as [GK24]. (5) Non-commutative cluster parameterizations of moduli spaces of G local systems. In particular, this gives a cluster parametrization of higher Teichmüller spaces for all groups admitting a positive structure with respect to Θ and a characterization of them as Jordan positive points. In the following we describe the results of this paper in more detail, starting with the structure theory of Jordan split groups G, describing the cluster structure and its applications for config-urations spaces of flags, for the group G and its special unipotent subgroup UΘ, and finally on the moduli spaces of decorated local systems on a surface S.1.1. Jordan split Lie groups and their structure theory. In the first part of the paper we develop the structure theory of semisimple algebraic groups defined over Jordan algebras. Given G, a semisimple algebraic group over a field K, and Θ ⊂ ∆, a subset of the set of positive simple roots ∆, we consider the associated parabolic subgroup PΘ, its Levi subgroup 

LΘ and unipotent subgroup UΘ along with their associated Lie algebras pΘ, lΘ and uΘ. The action of the center, zΘ, of lΘ on uΘ decomposes uΘ into weight spaces, giving rise to the parabolic root system associated to Θ.We say that a pair (G, Θ) induces a root system grading when this parabolic root system is a reduced root system R, whose Dynkin diagram we denote by ∆R. The parabolic Weyl group 

W (R) = N (ZΘ)/C(ZΘ), where ZΘ is the center of LΘ is the Weyl group of this reduced root system. The Lie algebras with an R-grading have been classified in [BM92, BZ96]. If (G, Θ) induces a root system grading with |Θ| = 1 then the Lie algebra g has an A1 grading which implies that UΘ is abelian. Lie algebras with an A1-grading are exactly the Lie algebras 

sl 2(J) which are built by applying the Tits-Kantor-Koecher construction to a Jordan algebra. In this situation, the vector space uΘ is the vector space underlying the Jordan algebra J. The group SL 2(J) is the simply connected algebraic group over K with Lie algebra sl 2(J). The list of Dynkin diagrams of reduced restricted root systems and choices of Θ which give such an A1

grading is given in Figure 3. Our first result is a classification of those (G, Θ) which induce root system gradings via a condition on the subset Θ. To state the classification, we introduce the following notion. Asubset Θ ⊂ ∆ is Jordan compatible if for each root β in Θ the connected subdiagram of 

∆ \ Θ ∪ { β} containing β is one of the diagrams associated to an A1-grading (see Figure 3). This in fact associates a Jordan algebra to each element of Θ.

Theorem 1.1. A pair (G, Θ) induces a root system grading if and only if Θ is Jordan compatible. 

Note that in particular (G, ∆) always induces a root system grading when G has a reduced root system. In this case each root space is associated with a Jordan division algebra, i.e. a6 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Jordan algebra where every nonzero element of V is mapped by ι to an automorphism. If 

∆R is not equal to ∆, then ∆R has to be of type Ap, B p, C p, F 4 or G2. In Theorem 3.35, we identify the possible classes of Jordan algebras that can appear in each type by studying the Jordan compatible subsets. For example, in type Ap with p > 2 each root corresponds to the same associative Jordan algebra. In type Bp, the short roots are assigned commutative Jordan algebras and the unique long root has a Jordan algebra of Clifford type. This is a reinterpretation of the results of [BM92, BZ96] in the classification of R graded Lie algebras. Next we reverse perspective, and initiate the study of groups (G, Θ) , for Θ Jordan compatible, as “split groups” defined over a family of Jordan algebras. For this we consider the root systems 

R as the scaffold that gives a prescribed way to “plug in” a collection of Jordan algebras {Ji}

which recreate a Lie algebra in a similar fashion to the Tits-Kantor-Koecher construction. Each 

R gives very strict rules for what collections {Ji} can be used in this construction, as outlined in Theorem 3.35. We call these groups Jordan split of type R and we define a Jordan pinning as a collection of homomorphisms SL 2(Ji) → G such that the image of the vector space of Ji lands in Uβi . We investigate the algebraic structure of Jordan pinnings and establish the following theorem. 

Theorem 1.2. The key algebraic relations among the images of Jordan pinnings depend only on the type of R and not the particular set of Jordan algebras {Ji}.

We call these algebraic relations the root system calculus for any Jordan split group G

of type R. In particular, we establish commutation and redecomposition relations among root systems and actions of the Weyl group both on the Levi and the root spaces. This is key for later applications. The pinning also identifies the parabolic subgroup PΘ with unipotent radical UΘ as the subgroup generated by the images of the positive roots. Furthermore, we deduce properties of the Bruhat decomposition of G with respect to PΘ, which mirror properties of the standard Bruhat decomposition with respect to the minimal parabolic subgroup. Here, the Weyl group 

W (R) gives the organizing principle. For example we prove: 

Theorem 1.3. For each word w ∈ W (R), there is a unique cell in the PΘ-Bruhat decomposition of G, PΘwP Θ. Moreover if w1, w 2 are two words in W (R) whose lengths add, 

PΘw1PΘw2PΘ = PΘw1w2PΘ .

We further explore the action of W (R) on the full Levi subgroup LΘ. For example we prove: 

Theorem 1.4. Fix a Jordan pinning of G by {Ji}. This induces coroot maps ˇβi : Γ( Ji) →

This realizes the asymmetry of a non simply laced uniformly across the choice of Jj .In the split case, the longest word in the Weyl group can be used to give a parametrization of an open and dense set in the unipotent radical of the Borel subgroup. This map was used prominently by Lusztig to give a parametrization of the totally positive unipotent semigroup. Here we give a Lusztig style parameterization of the unipotent subgroup UΘ using the longest word in W (R). For i a reduced expression of the longest word in W (R), the Lusztig map 

Fi : Ji1 × · · · JiN → UΘ

sends a list of invertible elements of the Jordan algebras onto an open dense subset of UΘ.This map is inspired by the parametrization of the positive unipotent semigroup with respect to a subset Θ ⊂ ∆ in the generalization of Lusztig’s total positivity introduced in [GW25]. Although the root system calculus and Jordan pinning only depend on the type of the root system, our later constructions will depend on the choice of Jordan algebras. We introduce NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 7

the noncommutative rank of Jordan split groups G of type R, which is the number of Ji

which are noncommutative Jordan algebras. The noncommutative rank is 0 precisely the split or quasi-split groups which were previously studied. It turns out there is an important distinction between higher noncommutative rank (greater than or equal to 2) and noncommutative rank 1. Noncommutative rank 1 will share many structural properties with rank 0 and we refer to the combined cases as lower noncommutative rank. We prove that non-quasi-split G are noncommutative rank 1 if and only if ∆R ≃ A1, B p, or G2 and higher non-commutative rank if and only if ∆R ≃ Ap, C p, or F4.The framework we develop gives a strong connection between groups of lower noncommutative rank and positive structures in Lie groups. When each Ji is a formally real Jordan algebra we say that G has a positive structure with respect to R. Given a Jordan pinning by formally real Jordan algebras, we define positive semigroups U >0Θ ⊂ UΘ as the image of the positive cones in each Ji under a Lusztig map for any reduced expression of the longest word in W (R). When 

K = R the pairs (G, Θ) which induce a positive structure on G with respect to some root system 

R are exactly those pairs (G, Θ) which admit a positive structure with respect to Θ as introduced in [GW25]. The positive semigroups are the very same positive semigroups as the ones defined in [GW25] and studied in [GW26a]. Our approach here gives several new insights, which we discuss in more detail below. 1.2. Non-commutative cluster varieties and applications. In the second part of the paper we use the structure theory of Jordan split groups of type R and a marked surface S to construct a noncommutative cluster variety AR,S . This noncommutative space is obtained by gluing together a collection of cluster charts. Each cluster chart consists of a noncommutative group of functions called the seed group along with some extra combinatorial data. The seed group is a free group modulo some simple relations among a set of distinguished monomials called angles .The seed group takes the place the ring of functions on a classical torus used to define a classic cluster variety. These cluster charts are glued together via mutations which are defined to have the property that the pullback of each angle is a (formal) sum of angles. These mutations are best understood via the Jordan points of the cluster variety AR,S . The restriction on the collection {Ji}

imposed by R identifies a single Jordan algebra J which contains each Ji. Then, a Jordan point of a cluster chart is a map from the seed group to the structure group Γ( J) of this Jordan algebra so that every angle Ai is mapped to ι(vi) for some vi ∈ V . Then the mutation of Jordan points is defined so that μ∗(A′) = ι(P vk). In this way, a Jordan point consist of elements of the group 

Γ( J), but the addition appearing in the mutation happens in the vector space V .Abelianinzing our construction recovers the A-cluster varieties of Fock and Goncharov [FG06] and of Goncharov and Shen [GS19]. In the Ap case this is the noncommutative cluster variety of Goncharov and Kontsevich [GK24] with some minor modification. In the Bp case this gives the noncommutative cluster variety structure conjectured in our previous work [GKNW26], while the G2 case is a small variation of this. The Cp case is entirely new, and we describe it by realizing it as a symmetric folding of the A2p−1 case. The F4 case and some exceptional cases are entirely new as well, and we do not explore all of their properties here. For any allowable choice of Jordan algebras {Ji}, AR,S has Jordan algebra points which parametrize G-local systems on Σ which preserve decorated Θ-flags, i.e. cosets in G/U Θ at the marked points. Considering the non-commutative cluster varieties for particularly simple surfaces, give us noncommutative cluster structures on G and the unipotent group UΘ. When 

(G, Θ) induces a positive structure, this cluster structure allows us to draw important conclu-sions. It can be used to give algebraic proofs of many of the properties of positive semigroups relative to Θ, and of positive configurations of flags. Moreover it gives us explicit positivity test, generalizing the well known positivity test from split real Lie groups [FG06, BFZ05]. Applied to the space of decorated G-local systems on Σ, the non-commutative cluster structures we define provide a unifying perspective of the A-theory of Fock and Goncharov for all groups admitting a positive structure, and thus for all (known) higher Teichmüller spaces associated to positive representations. 8 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

1.2.1. Flag varieties, configuration spaces, and cluster coordinates. The noncommutative cluster varieties AR,S associated to a Jordan-split type R and a marked surface S rely on the study of flag varieties and their configuration spaces as demonstrated in [FG06] and [GS19]. Given a Jordan split group G over a root system R there is an associated unipotent subgroup, UΘ,corresponding to the positive roots of R. We consider the decorated flag variety A := G/U Θ

and the configuration space Conf × 

> n

(A ) of pairwise transverse n-tuples of flags. The key point is to develop appropriate coordinate systems on Conf × 

> n

(A ).Many special cases of Jordan split groups have already been studied. For G = SL n(R) and 

R of type An−1, Fock and Goncharov [FG06] developed a set of coordinates for Conf × 

> n

(A ).General split real Lie groups G were studied in [GS19], where the authors describe a family of coordinate systems depending on a series of choices involving data from the root system. They then prove that these choices are a result of the cluster structure of Conf × 

> n

(A ).The case of G = Sp 2n(R) with an A1-grading given by the parabolic associated Θ = {αn},the unique long root, was studied in [AGRW21]. The case of G = SL kn with an An−1-grading induced by Θ = {k, 2k, · · · , (n − 1) k} was studied in [GK24]. In both these cases, a generalized noncommutative cluster structure was developed to describe the coordinates. However, the gen-eralizations appear very different, [AGRW21] are a realization of the noncommutative surfaces introduced by Berenstein and Retakh [BR18], while Goncharov and Kontsevich [GK24] used noncommutative plabic graphs. When the noncommutative rank is less than or equal to 1, the coordinates have the structure of a polygonal cluster algebra developed in [GKNW26]. Both the usual cluster algebra and noncommutative surface cluster algebras are special cases of polygonal cluster algebras. We review and slightly extend the notion of polygonal cluster algebra developed in [GKNW26] in Section 6. Polygonal cluster algebras come with a Weyl type, which can be A1, Bp, or G2, and a parameter r which can be any natural number for A1, has to be r = 2 for Bp and r = 3 for 

G2.In higher noncommutative rank, a different generalization is needed which we call grounded wiring networks . These networks are introduced in Section 7 and they include the networks used in [GK24]. We summarize our results in the following theorem. 

Theorem 1.5. Let K be a field which is not of characteristic 2 or 3. Let (G, Θ) be a Jordan split semisimple algebraic group over K with |∆R| =: p. Let Conf ×(AΘ) be the configuration space of decorated flags. Then there are cluster coordinates on Conf ×(AΘ), whose structure depend on the noncommutative rank of (G, Θ) .(1) When (G, Θ) is of non-commutative rank 0, the cluster coordinates are obtained by evaluating the commutative cluster varieties of [GS19] in field extensions of K.(2) When (G, Θ) is of non-commutative rank 1, the cluster coordinates are given by evalua-tions of non-commutative polygonal cluster algebras with general r for A1, r = 2 for Bp

and r = 3 for G2 in Jordan algebras. (3) When (G, Θ) is of higher non-commutative rank and of type Ap or Cp the cluster coor-dinates are given by evaluations of cluster algebras of grounded wiring networks of type 

Ap and Cp in Jordan algebras. 

1.2.2. Cluster structure on G, Gauss decompositions and positivity tests. When G is a split group and Θ = ∆ , Berenstein, Fomin and Zelevinsky described a cluster structure on double Bruhat cells in [BFZ05]. For two elements w1, w 2 ∈ W of the Weyl group, the double Bruhat cell is given by 

Cw1,w 2 := P∆w1P∆ ∩ P opp ∆ w2P opp ∆ .

The group G decomposes as a disjoint union of these cells. Moreover, if w0 ∈ W is the longest element, the cell 

Ce,w 0 = P∆ ∩ P opp ∆ w0P opp ∆

is open and dense in P∆. Thus, the cluster coordinates on Ce,w 0 describe an open dense subset of P∆. As explained in [Le19, Gil21] the cluster structure on Conf ×

When (G, Θ) admits a positive structure with respect to R, one can observe that these structures are compatible with the positive structure, which gives us the following theorems as corollaries. 

Theorem 1.7. The positive semigroup U >0Θ is contained in UΘ,0. The semigroup U >0Θ can be characterized as the set of positive J-points of the noncommutative polygonal cluster structure on UΘ,0. Moreover (1) Each cluster seed gives a precise set of positivity conditions, which are expressed as generalized minors of the group G of type R over the Jordan algebra J.(2) There is a sequence of cluster mutations, which give an explicit way to compute the change of coordinates between different Lusztig parameterizations of U >0Θ .Remark 1.8 . Note that getting explicit expression for the change of parameterizations without the cluster structure is quite hard in general. In [GW26b], explicit polynomial type equations are derived following the approach of Berenstein-Zelevinsky [BZ97] using the universal enveloping algebra, and formulas for change of coordinates were obtained with a computer assisted proof.

Moreover (1) Each cluster seed gives a precise set of positivity conditions, which are expressed as generalized minors of the group G of type R over the Jordan algebra J.(2) There is a sequence of cluster mutations for the flip of the quadrilateral, this implies in particular that G>0Θ = U >0Θ L◦

For the detailed formulas we refer the reader to Section 13. The generalized minors that appear in the positivity conditions are precisely the same as for the split case, interpreted in the right way when looking at G as group of type R over {Ji}.Whereas there is a lot of work on the nonnegative semigroups U ≥0 and G≥0 in the split case, much less is known for the nonnegative semigroups U ≥0Θ and G≥0Θ (see [Wie25] for some discus-sion). We expect the cluster parametrization to help understand the nonnegative semigroups. 1.2.3. Cluster structure on moduli spaces of flat bundles and higher Teichmüller spaces. The cluster structure on the configuration spaces of n-tuples of flags, allow us to deduce a cluster variety structure on certain moduli spaces of local systems on the surface S of negative Euler characteristic with marked points and punctures. This goes back to Penner’s work on decorated Teichmüller space [Pen87], but has been pioneered by Fock and Goncharov [FG06] in the case of split real Lie groups. They introduced a pair of positive varieties, the A-variety and the 

X -variety, and showed that their positive points correspond to higher Teichmüller spaces. We adapt their strategy to the situation of Jordan split groups G, focusing on the A-variety side of the story. We further explain that when G carries a positive structure these local systems are related to positive representations [GLW21, BGL +24]. Given a surface S with k-punctures and Jordan split group G of type R with associated parabolic subgroup PΘ, we consider the space of decorated (twisted) local systems on S, which depends on a representation ρ : π1(S) → G, with a decoration by an affine flag Ak, i.e. an element of G/U Θ for any puncture, which is fixed by the holonomy around this puncture. Building on the cluster structure on the space of configuration spaces, we build a cluster variety structure AR,S whose points parameterize the space of decorated local systems. 

Theorem 1.10. The Jordan points of the cluster variety AR,S parametrize an open and dense set of the space of decorated local systems. When G of type R carries a positive structure, the set of positive Jordan points, A>0 

> R,S

, of AR,S is independent of all choices and corresponds to the space of decorated positive representations of π1(S).

The cluster structure on the space of decorated local systems has strong consequences. (1) Given a curve γ on S, the holonomy along γ can be written explicitly in terms of the cluster coordinates. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 11 

(2) When G of type R is of non-commutative rank 1 and carries a positive structure, for any decorated local system in A>0 

> R,S

, the holonomy around any non-peripheral curve γ

is conjugate to an element in G>0Θ .(3) Since the mapping class group action on triangulations of S is generated by sequences of flips, the generalized mutations of the cluster structure gives explicit realizations of the action of the mapping class group. Expanding on this last point, we further consider the subgroup of the cluster modular group defined in [GS19]. This group, which we denote by ΓR,S , depends on the marked surface and the Jordan split type R of G; it is isomorphic to the group associated to the moduli space of decorated local systems for a split group of type R. It contains (1) The mapping class group of S.(2) The outer automorphisms of G which arise from automorphisms of the Dynkin diagram of R.(3) For each puncture on S, a copy of the Weyl group W (R).(4) For each boundary component, a copy of a sub-quotient of the Artin-Tits Braid group of type R.In the commutative setting, the first three actions act by usual cluster automorphisms, i.e. maps which send cluster variables to cluster variables, while the last acts by what are called clus-ter quasi-automorphisms, i.e. a cluster automorphism followed by a monomial transformation by frozen cluster variables. We define a noncommutative quasi-cluster automorphism to be a map which sends cluster variables to cluster variables followed by a noncommutative monomial transformation by frozen cluster variables. We show in Section 17.6 the following: 

Theorem 1.11. The group ΓR,S acts on AR,S by noncommutative (quasi) cluster automor-phisms. 

Fixing a noncommutative ring R, Goncharov and Kontsevich [GK24] give a spectral de-scription of the moduli space of fully twisted decorated rank m local systems on S. They show that this space is birationally equivalent to the space of twisted decorated local rank 1 local systems on a more complicated surface called the spectral surface which is associated to a particular plabic graph embedded on S.The noncommutative cluster charts we construct for Jordan split groups of type Ap are a natural generalization of those considered by Goncharov and Kontsevich, although there is a small difference in signs. This sign difference amounts to an untwisting of the local systems on the spectral surface. To this end we have: 

Theorem 1.12. The space AR,S is birationally equivalent to the moduli space of untwisted twisted rank 1 local systems on the spectral surface. 

When R is Cp, there is a spectral description of the the space AR,S in terms of local systems which are invariant under a natural symmetry of the spectral surface. 1.3. Structure of the paper. In Part 1, we discuss the structure of Lie algebras and Lie groups with a root system grading. This begins with a review of the theory of quadratic Jordan algebras in Section 2. In Section 3 we classify which choices of Θ induce root system gradings and compare the Weyl group of the grading to the original Weyl group of the restricted root system. Finally in Section 4, we introduce the notion of a Jordan pinning which we use to study the Θ-Bruhat decomposition and develop a “root system calculus” for computing in any group with a Jordan pinning. In Part 2, we provide the noncommutative generalizations of cluster varieties needed to pa-rameterizes configurations of flags and moduli spaces of local systems. We begin in Section 5 with a review of classic cluster algebra theory. In Section 6 we review the construction of polyg-onal cluster algebras we previously developed in [GKNW26]. Finally in Section 7, we introduce the notion of a grounded wiring network which provides the noncommutative cluster varieties in higher noncommutative rank. 12 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

In Part 3, we describe the construction of noncommutative cluster varieties which parameter-ize configurations of flags. Section 8 outlines the strategy from [GS19] and describes the basic invariant of a pair of decorated flags, the L-distance. Following the general strategy, Section 9 describes how to break a configuration of a triple of flags into elementary configurations. Then in Section 10, we construct noncommutative cluster varieties for any elementary configuration. The construction depends on the noncommutative rank of the Jordan-split group, noncommutative rank less than 1 is discussed in Section 10.2 and higher noncommutative rank in Section 10.3. In Section 11, we build the cluster varieties for triples of flags and in Section 12 we extend the construction to arbitrary tuples. In Section 13, we carefully apply the theory in each noncom-mutative rank 1 example ( A1, B p, G 2-gradings). Section 14 is the analogous section for higher noncommutative rank ( Ap, Cp, F4-gradings). In Part 4, we explore the natural applications of theory. First, we produce cluster-like coor-dinates on the algebraic groups themselves and their double Bruhat cells in Section 15. Next, in Section 16, we explore positivity and the natural semigroup structures that arise when all the Jordan algebras are Euclidean (formally real). In Section 17, we describe how to extend the cluster varieties for tuples of flags to decorated twisted local systems. The positive points of these systems are closely related to the higher Teichmüller spaces of surfaces. 

# Part 1. Root-graded Lie Algebras and Algebraic Groups 

In this part we develop the theory of Jordan split groups. First, we provide the necessary background on quadratic Jordan algebras. 2. Quadratic Jordan algebras 

Let K be a field, not of characteristic 2 (usually we take R or C). 

Definition 2.1. A quadratic Jordan algebra , J = ( V, ι, 1), consists of a vector space V over 

K, a quadratic map ι : V → End( V ), and a special element 1 ∈ V such that (1) ι(1) is the identity transformation, (2) ι ι(a)( b) = ι(a)ι(b)ι(a),and defining ι(a, b )( c) :=  ι(a + c) − ι(a) − ι(c)(b),(3) ι(x)ι(y, x ) = ι(x, y )ι(x).That ι is quadratic means by definition that the map bι : V × V → End( V ), defined by 

bι(v, w ) := ι(v + w) − ι(v) − ι(w)

is bilinear. This implies that ι(·, ·) is a bilinear map (which actually maps to End( V )), and thus induces a linear map V ⊗ V → End( V ).

Lemma 2.2. The map ι(·, ·) determines the map ι.Proof. Using that K is not characteristic 2 we find 

ι(a)( c) = 12 ι(a, c )( a) .

□

Remark 2.3 . A classical Jordan algebra over K is a vector space over K with a commutative but non associative product V ◦ V → V satisfying some axioms. The classical Jordan product is recovered from the quadratic definition as a ◦ b := 12 ι(a, b )( 1). On the other hand, the classical product determines the map ι by 

ι(a)( b) = 2 a ◦ (a ◦ b) − (a ◦ a) ◦ b. 

Thus the two theories are the same when the characteristic of the underlying field K is not 2. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 13 

Example 2.4 . Let R be a (possibly noncommutative) K-algebra. We denote by J(R) the Jordan algebra with vector space given by R, ι(a)( b) = aba and 1 = 1 R. We call such a Jordan algebra 

associative type .If R is equipped with an anti-involution † : R → R then R† denotes the subset of elements fixed by the anti-involution. There is a Jordan subalgebra H(R, †) ⊂ J(R) with vector space 

R† ⊂ R , and ι(a)( b) = aba †. We call these Jordan algebras Hermitian type .

Example 2.5 . Let R be a commutative K-algebra and M be an R-module with a bilinear form 

b : M ⊗M → R . Define rw(v) = 12 b(w, w )v −b(v, w )w and fix an element e ∈ M with b(e, e ) = 1 .We define a Jordan algebra J(M, b, e ) by ι(m)( n) = rm(re(n)) . One easily checks that e acts as the identity element and ι satisfies the axioms for a Jordan algebra. We call these Jordan algebras Clifford type as they are subalgebras of the associative Jordan algebra made from the Clifford algebra associated to (M, b ).

Example 2.6 . Let O be an octonion algebra over K, e.g. made from the Cayley-Dickson con-struction [Dic19]. Let H3(O) be the Jordan algebra with V given by Hermitian 3 × 3-matrices over O, ι defined most easily by using the classical Jordan product a ◦ b = 12 (ab + ba ) and Remark 2.3, and 1 given by the identity matrix. Such a Jordan algebra is called an Albert algebra and is 27-dimensional over K.

Definition 2.7. A Jordan algebra is called special if it is isomorphic to a subalgebra of J(R)

for some ring R. We call a Jordan algebra commutative if it is special with R commutative and noncommutative otherwise. When J is special there is a minimal specializing ring which contains it called the universal specializing envelope .A proof of the following theorem is given in [McC04, Appendix B]. 

Theorem 2.8 (Shirshov–Cohn) . Any Jordan algebra with 2 generators is special. 

All Jordan algebras are special, other than the 27-dimensional exceptional Albert algebras. Over an algebraically closed field there is one exceptional Albert algebra. 2.1. The inner structure group. Definition 2.9. An element a ∈ V is called invertible if its image ι(a) is invertible and there exists b ∈ V such that ι(a)b = a. We write V × for the subset of invertible elements. When all nonzero elements are invertible, the Jordan algebra is called a Jordan division algebra .The element a−1 := b is unique when it exists. 

Definition 2.10. The inner structure group Inn( J) is the subgroup of GL( V ) generated by the image of ι(V ×). The inner automorphism group Aut( J) is the subgroup of Inn( J) which fixes 1 ∈ V .Generally, the map ι is clearly not injective since ι(a) = ι(−a). However, we can construct a group Γ( J) which is a covering Γ( J) → Inn( J), so that there exists a map eι : V × → Γ( J) that extends ι and is injective, we will see this in Section 4.1. When J is special, there is an action of the group R× × R × on R by (a, b ) · r = arb −1.The elements {(z, z )|z ∈ Z(R)} act trivially. The inner structure group is the subgroup of 

R× × R ×/Z (R) which is generated by the image of ι(v) = ( v, v −1) for v ∈ V ×.

Definition 2.11. The structure involution , σ : Inn( J) → Inn( J), is the anti-involution defined on generators by σ(ι(v)) = ι(v) so that if g = ι(v1) . . . eι(vr ) we have σ(g) = eι(vr ) . . . eι(v1).One checks that this anti-involution is well-defined. Notice that the structure involution satisfies that for A ∈ Inn( J) we have ι(A(v)) = Aι (v)σ(A).For special algebras, the structure involution is induced by σ(a, b ) = ( b−1, a −1).2.2. Real Jordan algebras. The Jordan algebras over K = R are classified using the classifi-cation of finite dimensional algebras over R. We recall the classification here. We denote by D any of the rings R, C, the quaternions H, the split complex numbers, Cspl ,or the split quaternions, Hspl .14 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Theorem 2.12 ([McC04]) . A simple real Jordan algebra belongs (up to isotopy) to (at least) one of the following families: 

• (Associative type ) J is isomorphic to Mn(D), the Jordan algebra of n × n-matrices. 

• (Hermitian type ) J is isomorphic to Hn(D), the Jordan algebra of Hermitian matrices. Note that when D = H or Hspl , there are two involutions which can be used to identify the set of Hermitian matrices, standard conjugation and (a+bi+cj+dk)∗ = ( a+bi+cj−dk).We write H∗

> n

(H) for the Jordan algebra with the second form. 

• (Albert type ) J can be isomorphic to H3(O) or H3(Ospl ), Hermitian matrices over the octonions or split-octonions. 

• (Clifford type ) For s > 0 and r ≥ 0, J can be isomorphic to J(s, r ) the Jordan algebra of Clifford type whose vector space has a signature (s, r ) quadratic form. 

These real Jordan algebras serve as key examples in the remainder of the paper, so it is critical to understand their structure. First, we observe the connection between the structure groups of Hermitian and associative type Jordan algebras. When there is no confusion, we will refer to M1(D) simply by D. For 

Hn(D), the vector space is the set of n × n matrices over D which are fixed by an involution 

†. The structure group can be identified with GL n(D) which acts on V by M 7 → AM A †. The structure involution is simply † in this case. The corresponding associative type algebra Mn(D) has structure group S(GL n(D) × GL n(D)) 

⊂ GL n(D) × GL n(D). To compare the structure group, we embed GL n(D) in GL n(D) × GL n(D)

via A 7 → (A, A −† ). This preserves the action of GL n(D) on Hn(D) when viewed as a sub-Jordan algebra of Mn(D).Next we observe that when n ≤ 2, the Hn(O) and Hn(Ospl ) are actually isomorphic to algebras in the other families: 

H1(O) ≃ R, H1(Ospl ) ≃ R, H2(O) ≃ J(1 , 9) , H2(Ospl ) ≃ J(5 , 5) .

Therefore, these Jordan algebras are special. However, for n = 3 the Albert type algebras are not special and their inner structure group is different from the other cases. Instead the inner structure groups are related to central extensions of the real Lie groups E(−26) 6 or E(6) 6

respectively. There are several other exceptional isomorphisms between small members of each family. 

Example 2.13 . There are several examples involving the split division algebras, Cspl and Hspl :

2.3. Isotopy and norm maps. We will generally be interested in a slightly weaker notion of isomorphism of Jordan algebras called isotopy .

Definition 2.14. Let u be an invertible element of J = ( V, ι, 1). The u-isotope of J is the Jordan algebra J(u) = ( V, ι (u), u −1) where ι(u)(x) = ι(x)ι(u). We write J ∼ J′ if there is an isotopy J to J′.Isotopy is different from isomorphism, which requires that the identity element is sent to the identity element. If u has a square root, u 12 , then the the element of the inner structure group 

ι(u− 12 ) provides an isomorphism J → J(u). However, if u does not have a square root then there is no isomorphism between J and its u-isotope. 

Definition 2.15. The twisted forms of J are the isotopy classes of J modulo isomorphism. For a vector space V , let Sym r (V ∗) denote the rth symmetric power of the dual vector space. 

Definition 2.16. We say a quadratic Jordan algebra has a norm map of degree r if it is equipped with a map N : V → K which satisfies (1) N (v) = eN (v, . . . , v ) for some eN ∈ Sym r (V ∗),(2) N (1) = 1 ,(3) v ∈ V × if and only if N (v)̸ = 0 ,(4) N (ι(v)( w)) = N (v)2N (w) .In Section 4.1, we will see the norm map lifts to a character of a cover of Inn( J) defined on generators by N (ι(v)) = N (v).

Proposition 2.17 ([McC65]) . Every (finite dimensional) Jordan algebra is normed of degree r

for some r.Proof. In [McC65], they construct a generic norm for any finite dimensional Jordan using a generic minimal polynomial. □

Note that in the u-isotope, we have N (u)(x) = N (x)N (u).

Definition 2.18. Every degree r normed Jordan algebra has an adjugate map , τ : V × → V ×

defined by τ (v) = v−1N (v).The adjugate gives an anti-involution on (a cover of) Inn( J), defined on generators by 

τ (ι(v)) = N (v)ι(v)−1 .

Example 2.19 .

• For J = Mn(R) a norm of degree n is given by the determinant. 

• If J = J(s, r ) is a real Jordan algebra of Clifford type, a norm of degree 2 is given by the quadratic form q. On the inner structure group Inn( J) = Γ 0(s, r )/±1 the norm can be expressed as v1 · · · vk 7 → q(v1) · · · q(vk), using that any element in the Clifford group can be written as a product of vectors. Using the defining relation of the Clifford algebra, 

vv = q(v), the adjugate map is given by the Clifford transpose, τ (v1 · · · vk) = vk · · · v1.

Remark 2.20 . The adjugate map can be extended to a map on all of V satisfying ι(v)( τ v ) = 

N (v)v. We will not need to take the adjugate of non-invertible elements in which case the definition via the norm is sufficient. When K is formally real we can define a notion of a positivity on a Jordan algebra. 

Definition 2.21. A positive structure on a quadratic Jordan algebra over a formally real field K is a subset V >0 ⊂ V × called the positive cone which is compatible with the Jordan algebra structure as follows: (1) V >0 is closed under addition and scalar multiplication by positive elements of K.(2) V >0 contains 1 and is preserved by the action of Inn( J).(3) V >0 ⊂ V ×.(4) V >0 is closed under inverse. 16 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD

Euclidean Jordan algebras .

We remark that each simple complex Jordan algebra has a unique real form (up to isotopy) which is Euclidean. 2.4. Tits-Kantor-Koecher Lie algebra. Our philosophy is that the Lie algebra sl 2(J) encodes all of the properties of J which are invariant under isotopy. In Section 3.4, we will give a combinatorial classification for which Lie algebras can be realized as sl 2(J) for some Jordan algebra. 3. Lie algebras graded by root systems 

We now describe an additional structure on a Lie algebra, called a grading by a root system, which allows us to realize the algebra as a “split algebra” over a smaller root system. The simplest case, an A1 grading, is equivalent to realizing the algebra as sl 2(J) for some Jordan algebra. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 17 

3.1. Preliminaries. We first provide some background on abstract root systems, see [Kna96]. Let E be a Euclidean space with inner product denoted (·, ·). We define for v, w ∈ E

⟨v, w ⟩ := 2 (v, w )(w, w ) .

Recall the definition of a root system: 

Definition 3.1. A finite subset R ⊂ E \ { 0} is called a root system if (1) R spans E.(2) For α ∈ R, the reflection sα defined by sα(v) = v − ⟨ v, α ⟩α, leaves R invariant. (3) For α, β ∈ R, ⟨β, α ⟩ ∈ Z.We call dim( E) =: rk( R) the rank of the root system R. The subgroup of GL( E) generated by the reflections sα for α ∈ R is called the Weyl group of R. The root system R is called 

reduced if the only scalar multiples of a root α which are contained in R are α and −α.A subset ∆ = {α1, · · · , α p} ⊂ R is called a set of simple roots if it is a basis of E such that any element α ∈ R can be written as a linear combination with integer coefficients all of which are ≥ 0 or all of which are ≤ 0. It is a standard fact that a root system admits a set of simple roots. This defines sets of positive and negative roots, depending on the sign of the coefficients. We use the notation [α : αi] for the coefficient of αi in the decomposition of α so that α = P[α : αi]αi.

Definition 3.2. Given a set of simple roots, every other root α has a well defined height given by P[α : αi]. There is a unique root with maximum height called the highest root , αM .The highest root is also the maximum element under the partial order given by α > β if and only if α − β is a nonnegative sum of simple roots. This further implies that for every simple root αi and root α, [αM : αi] ≥ [α : αi]. The simple roots are the minimal positive roots under this order. 

Lemma 3.3. For each simple root i, there is an (non-unique) ascending chain of roots αi =

β1, · · · , β k = αM such that βj − βj−1 is a simple root. Moreover if β0 = 0 , each simple root α

appears [αM : α] many times as βj − βj−1.Proof. The chain can be built inductively. By [Kac84, Proposition 4.9], the highest root is the unique root such that αM + αi is not a root for any αi. So for each βj there is some simple root 

α so βj+1 = βj + α is a root. We observe that at step j, the coefficient, [βj : αi] is the number of times αi was used. Applying this to the final step, βk = αM proves the final statement. □

The reflections in the simple roots generate the Weyl group. The length of an element w in the Weyl group is the minimal number of simple reflections required to write w. The longest word in the Weyl group, w0 is the unique element with maximal length. It sends all positive roots to negative roots. The action of −w0, fixes the set of simple roots and is called the 

opposition involution .Finally, if we fix a set of simple roots ∆ = {α1, . . . , α p}, we obtain a p × p-matrix C with entries 

cij = ⟨αi, α j ⟩

which is called the Cartan matrix of R. This defines the coroots ˇαi ∈ E∗ in the dual of E

via the condition 

ˇαi(αj ) = cij .

The Weyl group acts on E∗ via the dual maps of the reflections sα, and we thus obtain the dual root system ˇR ⊂ E∗. In particular, we obtain a coroot associated to any root, not just the simple ones. Importantly, root systems are used to study Lie algebras: Let G be a reductive group over 

K and g its Lie algebra. Let T be a maximal split K-torus in G, t its Lie algebra. The adjoint action of t, or equivalently T , decomposes g into root spaces as 

g = g0 ⊕ M

> α∈R

gα18 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

for some subset R ⊂ t∗, called the restricted root system of (G, T ). For any t ∈ t and x ∈ gα,we have [t, x ] = α(t)x. The subset R is an abstract root system in t∗ where the inner product comes from endowing t with the Killing form. The Weyl group of (G, T ) is defined as W (G, T ) := NG(T )/CG(T ) where NG(T ) is the normalizer of T in G, CG(T ) is the centralizer. This acts by conjugation on T , and via the induced action on t by reflections on R. In fact W (G, T ) is isomorphic to the Weyl group of the abstract root system. For the subsequent discussion it is useful to interpret the roots as characters of T , i.e. as ele-ments of Hom( T, K×), rather than as linear maps t → K. The latter arise simply as differentials of the former at the identity in T .For each root α we write Tα ⊂ T for the kernel of the root α, Gα := CG(Tα) for the centralizer of Tα, and Sα := [ Gα, G α] is its derived subgroup. Sα ∩ T is the image of the coroot 

ˇα : K× → G. Just like the roots, the coroots can be viewed in different ways: If we interpret the roots as elements of t∗, the coroots are elements of t or equivalently of Hom( K, t). Viewing the roots as characters of T instead, the coroots should be thought of as cocharacters, i.e. elements of Hom( K×, T ), which is the perspective we will usually take. If we pick a set of simple roots 

{a1, . . . , α r }, the simple coroots are defined by 

αi ◦ ˇαj = ( k 7 → kcji ) ∈ Aut( K×) .

Fix a choice of simple roots ∆ and write R+ for the set of positive roots. To each choice 

Θ ⊂ ∆ we associate a standard parabolic subgroup PΘ. It is the subgroup whose Lie algebra is given by 

pΘ := g0 ⊕ M

> α∈R+

gα

M

> α∈Span(∆ \Θ) ∩R+

g−α .

The opposite standard parabolic P opp Θ is obtained by switching the roles of the positive and negative roots. The Levi subgroup , LΘ, is defined to be PΘ ∩ P opp Θ . The center of the Levi subgroup is denoted ZΘ. The unipotent subgroups UΘ and U opp Θ are the unipotent radicals of PΘ and P opp Θ respectively. We have PΘ ≃ UΘ ⋊ LΘ.The subgroups defined above are called standard; any other parabolic subgroup is conjugate in G to some PΘ.

In the next section we will understand when the groups uβi behave like the simple root spaces of a root space decomposition of different root system than the one of (G, T ).3.2. Root Grading. We have seen that the adjoint action of a maximal split torus on a Lie algebra decomposes it into root spaces. We now consider a similar decomposition, where we start with an abstract root system. 

Definition 3.5. Let R be a reduced root system. A Lie algebra g is graded by R if it has a decomposition 

g = M

> β∈R∪{ 0}

gβ

such that (1) g is generated as a Lie algebra by gβ for β ∈ R. In particular every element of g0 is given by a sum of iterated brackets of elements in the other graded summands. (2) The split Lie algebra gspl with root system R is a subalgebra of g such that there is a choice of a split Cartan subalgebra h ⊂ gspl such that gspl

⊂ gβ for all β ∈ R ∪ { 0}.(3) The adjoint of action of h on gβ is the diagonal action with eigenvalue given by β.Symbolically, 

∀h ∈ h ⊂ g0, ∀g ∈ gβ , [h, g ] = β(h) · g . R-graded Lie algebras were introduced in [BM92], who classified all Lie algebras which can be graded by simply laced root systems of rank at least 2. Later, [BZ96] extended this classification to all reduced finite root systems and beyond. Given a choice of Chevalley generators for gspl , we obtain an element 1β ∈ gβ with the following property: If z ∈ gγ then

3.4. A1-grading. There is a simple classification of A1-gradings. We will show in Theorem 3.25 that all A1 gradings are induced by a parabolic subgroup P{α} for some simple root α as in Definition 3.7. Moreover, there are explicit necessary and sufficient conditions on α for P{α}

to induce an A1-grading. All possible choices of α that result in an A1-grading are shown in Figure 3 by the circled node. 

A2n−1 : n − 1 n − 1

Bn : Cn : Dn :

D∗ 

> 2n

: E7 :

Figure 3. Choice of Θ = {α} giving an A1-grading. First, we classify 3-graded simple Lie algebras, which are almost A1-graded. 

Definition 3.19. A Lie algebra g is 3-graded if g decomposes as g−1 ⊕ g0 ⊕ g1 such that 

[g0, gi] ⊂ gi, [g1, g1] = 0 , [g−1, g−1] = 0 , [g1, g−1] ⊂ g0 .

A 3-grading gives the appropriate decomposition for an A1-grading, but does not guarantee the existence of a “good” split A1-subalgebra satisfying Property 3 of a root grading. 

Lemma 3.20. Let g be a simple Lie algebra. Then g is 3-graded if and only if there is a simple root α such that the parabolic subgroup P{α} induces the grading. Moreover the coefficient of α

in the longest root, [αM : α] = 1 .Proof. We first show that when [αM : α] = 1 , P{α} induces a 3-grading. By Lemma 3.8, the eigenspaces of the decomposition are indexed by the possible coefficients of α for any root. Since 

[αM : α] = 1 , there are three options {− 1, 0, 1}, splitting g = g−1 ⊕g0 ⊕g1. Lemma 3.8 also states that each gi further splits into root spaces, so it suffices to understand the brackets between root spaces to check the bracket relations for 3-grading. For example, to check [g1, g1] = 0 it suffices to consider the bracket between gβ ⊂ g1 and gγ ⊂ g1. The coefficient [β + γ : α] = [ β :

α] + [ γ : α] = 2 and so β + γ is not a root and [gβ , gγ ] = 0 as needed. The remaining bracket relations follow from analogous calculations of coefficients of α among root spaces living in the corresponding graded pieces. For the converse, suppose that g = g−1 ⊕ g0 ⊕ g1 is a 3-grading. We can choose the maximal split torus, t, to be contained in g0 so that the root space decomposition refines the 3-grading. Therefore we can choose a set of simple roots so that the subalgebra p = g0 ⊕ g1 is a standard parabolic subalgebra. Let Θ be the subset of simple roots which do not vanish uniformly on t ∩ z. Since g1 is nonempty, Θ is nonempty as well. If |Θ| > 1 then let α1, α 2 be distinct simple roots in Θ.Consider a chain from α1 to the highest root αM . Since [αM , α 2] > 0 there is a positive root on the chain α′ with [α′ : α2] = 0 and α′ + α2 a root. The root space gα′ is in g1 and has nontrivial bracket with gα2 ⊂ g1 contradicting the 3-grading. Similarly if [αM : α] > 1, there is a positive root on the chain from α1 to αM with [α′ : α1] = 1 and α′ + α1 a root. The same contradiction applies in this case. Therefore the 3-grading is induced by p{α} with [αM : α] = 1 

as claimed. □

In order to extend the classification of 3-gradings to A1-gradings, we need to build a good sl 2

triple in g compatible with the 3-grading. This requires some explicit computations using the highest root in a finite root system. The coefficients of the highest root in a root system R are related to a special labeling for the nodes of the Dynkin diagram of the associated affine root system , eR. This Dynkin diagram for eR can be obtained by adding one node, called the extending node , and edges to the Dynkin diagram for R. We refer to [Kac84] for details about affine root systems and this labeling. 

Lemma 3.21 ([Kac84], Proposition 4.7) . The Dynkin diagrams of an affine root system are characterized by the existence of a labeling of the nodes by positive integers such that the smallest 24 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

label is 1 and twice each label is the sum of the adjacent labels “with multiplicity.” Equivalently, there is a positive integer vector (d1, · · · , d p) in the cokernel of the Cartan matrix of eR whose minimal entry is 1, P cij di = 0 .

Removing a node labeled 1 from the Dynkin diagram of eR results in a labeled Dynkin diagram for R. Let di be the label of the node corresponding to αi. This is independent of the choice of node labeled 1 to remove. 

Lemma 3.22 ([Kac84], Remark 4.9) . The coefficient [αM : αi] of the highest root is equal to the label di.

We now decompose the longest word in the Weyl group, w0, into commuting reflections about positive roots. This decomposition will help build the split A1-subalgebra inside g. Recall from Definition 3.1, that each root, α, defines a reflection , sα, by sα(v) = v − ⟨ v, α ⟩α.

Lemma 3.23. Let X be a reduced root system and r be the number of orbits of simple roots under the opposition involution. There exists a family of r strongly orthogonal roots δ1, · · · , δ r

such that w0 = Q sδi and δi is the highest root of a connected subdiagram of Xi.Proof. We begin with δ1 = P diαi the highest positive root. The other δi will be constructed inductively as the highest roots of subdiagrams fixed by reflection in all previous roots. Now we consider the action of δ1 on αj an arbitrary simple root,

Theorem 3.25. Let g be a simple Lie algebra. Then g is A1-graded if and only if there is a root α such that P{α} induces the grading. Moreover, α is fixed by the opposition involution and has [αM : α] = 1 .Proof. We begin by showing that whenever α is fixed by the opposition involution and [αM :

and α′ + β a root. Moreover [α′ : α] = 1 so gα′+β ∈ g1 and g−α′ ∈ g−1. Then [gα′+β , g−α′ ] = gβ

as needed. Next we check there is a good split A1 subalgebra in g satisfying Properties 2 and 3. Con-cretely, we find an sl 2 triple (E, F, H ) with E ∈ g1, F ∈ g−1, and H ∈ t ∩ z{α} ⊂ g0. We build

Theorem 3.28. Let Θ ⊂ ∆ be a subset of simple roots and pΘ the associated parabolic subalge-bra. Then the grading induced by pΘ is a grading by a root system of some type Y if and only if Θ is Jordan compatible. Proof. First we assume that pΘ induces a root grading of type Y . Consider the subalgebra generated by the roots in the subdiagram Fi corresponding to βi. The restriction of PΘ to this subalgebra induces an A1-grading and thus is given by a parabolic associated to a root γi with 

[αMi : γi] = 1 for αMi the highest root in Fi and γi fixed by the opposition involution in Fi.Moreover this parabolic is the restriction of PΘ and so γi = βi as needed for Θ to be Jordan compatible. We now show the converse. Assume Θ is Jordan compatible and thus that each diagram Fj

has an induced A1-grading. This gives elements 1±βj for each simple root in Θ as the element 

E constructed from a sum of strongly orthogonal root spaces as in Theorem 3.25. The parabolic 

pΘ divides g into the union of root spaces which agree on h = zΘ ∩ t. Thus each graded piece is labeled by a Y -root, the projection the restricted root space onto the subspace spanned by Θ.It remains to check this decomposition satisfies the three properties of a root grading. Property 1 of being Y -graded follows directly from the A1-grading on each subdiagram. The sums of iterated brackets of 1±βj generate the part of the Levi subgroup corresponding to each diagram. Therefore all of the Levi ( g0) is generated by brackets in the proposed Y -roots. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 27 

Property 2 is the bulk of the proof and requires a case by case check that the subalgebra generated by the identity elements 1±βj is a split Lie algebra of type Y . This subalgebra will have Cartan subspace h.Property 3 is then an easy consequence of the parabolic grading. The action of the split torus, h, acts by the Y -roots on each restricted root space, not only on K1β as needed. We now focus on Property 2 and check the identity elements generate a split subalgebra. In order to determine the type of the split subalgebra we focus on the subdiagram Fij = Fi ∪ Fj

β1 decomposes into twice as many root spaces as β2. Each root of β2 pairs nontrivially with two roots of β1 with value −1. The asymmetry in sizes results in [h1, 1β2 ] = −21β2 while

The finiteness of the Serre relation, ad( 1βi )−⟨ βj ,β i⟩+1 (1βj ) = 0 , is clear by observing the coefficients of the nodes in Θ in the highest root. In each connected case, the coefficients of 

(β1, β 2) are (1 , 1) , (2 , 1) , or (3 , 2) when the subdiagram is type A2, B 2 or G2 respectively. Since the adjoint action maps root spaces to root spaces, the maximum numbers of times ad( 1βi ) can be nonzero is the coefficient, [αM : βi]. Moreover, [αM : βi] is the coefficient of the highest root in a root system of type Y . For example, in F4(2) , the highest root is αM = 23 ⇒ 43 = 2α1 + 3 α2 + 4 α3 + 2 α4. Then β1 = 10 ⇒ 00 and [αM : β1] = 2 and β2 = 01 ⇒ 00 has 

[αM : β2] = 3 matching G2 as needed. Moreover these coefficients match the coefficients of the highest root of the relevant root system. This implies that these final Serre relations match the proposed root system.

There is one special folding of B3 onto G2, which does not arise from an automorphism of the diagram. Instead, the symmetry identifies the root spaces corresponding to the two ends. This makes sense as it “finishes” the folding of D4 onto G2 that began by folding D4 onto B3.

Proposition 3.29. Let R be a root system which folds onto a root system R′. Then any Lie algebra which is R-graded is also R′-graded. Proof. The root spaces of the folded algebra are given by the orbits of the symmetry used to fold it. Thus, the R′-grading is given by identifying the pieces of the R-grading under the folding symmetry. □

Proposition 3.30. Every R-grading of a simple Lie algebra comes from a grading by a parabolic subgroup possibly followed by a folding. Proof. Suppose g is simple and R-graded. By the recognition theorems (Section 3.3) we can assume that g has a reduced restricted root system (i.e. is not of type BC ), as otherwise it would not be graded by a reduced root system. Denote by R+ the set of positive roots in R. Consider the associated parabolic subalgebra 

p = L 

> β∈R+∪{ 0}

gβ . We wish to prove that p induces a grading by a root system R′ which comes with a folding R′ → R. Write z for the center of g0. We have h ⊂ z where h is the Cartan subgroup of the split group coming from the R-grading. Consider a simple R-root space gβ .The action of z on it decomposes it into gβ = L 

> i

gγi , since gβ is abelian these commute with each other. As p is a parabolic subalgebra, each γi corresponds to a root αi of g.Elements of the normalizer of h must map g0 into itself, and hence must map z into itself as well. Therefore the W (R) action respects the decomposition of gβ by z, i.e. any root space for a root in the W (R) orbit of β also decomposes into the same number of z-root spaces. In particular g−β decomposes into L 

> i

g−γi . Since these commute with each other, we have that 

[gγi , g−γj ] = 0 for i̸ = j as well. Now we observe that the projection of the identity elements 1±β into the g±γi give sl 2-triples in gγi ⊕ g−γj ⊕ [gγi , g−γj ]. This implies that these subalgebras are A1-graded and hence the collection of roots αi must be Jordan compatible so that this parabolic induces a root grading 

R′.The folding from R′ to R is given by grouping all the simple γj whose root spaces are contained in the same gβ . □

3.7. Identifying Jordan factors. Our classification of Jordan compatible subsets of roots of a Dynkin diagram (Theorem 3.28) recovers most of the features of the recognition theorems given in Section 3.3. In particular, if g is a simple Lie algebra over K and the parabolic subalgebra associated to a subset Θ ⊂ ∆ induces an R-grading we obtain restrictions on the possible Jordan algebras associated to the roots in R.First, we reinterpret what we have established about A1-graded algebras to give a classification analogous to the classification of Jordan algebras over an algebraically closed field given in [McC04, Renaissance Structure Theorem]. 

Proposition 3.31. Let g be an A1-graded simple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field 

Kalg . Then g = sl 2(J) and belongs to one of the following cases: 

• Associative type: sl 2(J) is type A2n−1(1) and J ∼ Mn(Kalg ).

• Clifford type: sl 2(J) is type Bn(1) /D n(1) and J ∼ J(V, b ),for V a 2n − 1 or 2n − 2 dimensional vector space and b a nondegenerate form on V .

• Symmetric Hermitian type: sl 2(J) is type Cn(1) and J ∼ Hn(Kalg ).

• Quaternionic Hermitian type: sl 2(J) is type D∗

> 2n

(1) and J ∼ Hn(D),for D the split quaternions over Kalg .

• Exceptional Type: sl 2(J) is type E7(1) and J ∼ H3(O),for O the split octonions over Kalg .Proof. By Proposition 3.30, every A1-grading is induced by a parabolic subgroup. This parabolic subgroup corresponds to an isotopy class of Jordan algebras over Kalg . Since Kalg is algebraically closed, there is one simple Lie algebra for each Dynkin diagram in our classification of parabolic subgroups which induce A1-gradings (Theorem 3.25). □30 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

In order to extend the result to non algebraically closed K we need to recall the classification of simple Lie algebras by the Satake-Tits Index :

Definition 3.32. Suppose g is a Lie algebra over K with restricted root system ∆. We write 

∆alg for the Dynkin diagram of galg := g ⊗K Kalg over the algebraic closure Kalg . There is a subset ∆• ⊂ ∆alg of roots whose restriction to the maximal torus of g is trivial. There is also an induced action, ⋄, of the Galois group Gal( Kalg /K) on the non trivial roots, ∆◦ := ∆ alg \ ∆•.The triple (∆ alg , ∆◦, ⋄) is called the Satake-Tits index of g.

Lemma 3.33. Suppose g has restricted root system A1. Then g ≃ sl 2(J) for J a division Jordan algebra over K. Also, the Satake-Tits index is such that ∆alg is a product of identical copies of identical A1 graded diagrams, ∆◦ is the set of A1 grading nodes, and ⋄ acts transitively on ∆◦.Proof. The fact that J is a division Jordan algebra is proved later by analyzing the Θ-Bruhat decomposition of g. Since g has restricted root system A1, we will see that the corresponding Jordan algebra has capacity 1 (see Definition 3.43) and so every nonzero element is in the top Bruhat cell by Proposition 4.14. Then Proposition 4.39 will show that all of these elements are invertible. For the second part, first assume that ⋄ acts trivially. In this case ∆◦ must be one element, its easy to see from the definition of the Tits index that this induces an A1-grading on galg .If the ⋄ action is nontrivial, we can first pass to the smallest extension of K which makes the 

⋄ action trivial, denote it by K′. The number of embeddings of this field into Kalg gives the number of copies of this A1 graded diagram which are permuted by the ⋄ action. □

We call J associative, Clifford, Hermitian, or exceptional type depending on the corresponding type of the Jordan algebra Jalg obtained by extending scalars. 

Lemma 3.34. If ∆ is a reduced root system, then galg is also ∆-graded. Moreover Θ = ∆ ◦ is a Jordan compatible subset of ∆alg . The ⋄ action descends to ∆alg Θ , folding by this action gives the ∆-grading. Proof. Since · ⊗ K Kalg commutes with direct sum, the ∆-grading on galg is given by applying 

· ⊗ K Kalg to each graded piece of g. The definition of the Satake-Tits index implies that the center of the Levi of the parabolic subgroup determined by Θ contains the maximal split torus of g. We can see that the maximal split torus is exactly the fixed points of the Galois group. Thus if the star action is trivial, then each node in ∆◦ corresponds directly to a node in ∆, each of which correspond to A1 graded subalgebras, which implies that ∆◦ is Jordan compatible. In the case that the ⋄ action is nontrivial, each orbit must correspond to a number of copies of the same A1 graded subalgebras. This gives a folding of ∆alg Θ . □

The previous lemma allows us to prove an “identification theorem” for any R-graded algebra which identifies which Jordan algebras can appear in each type. 

Theorem 3.35 (Identification) . Let g be a semisimple R-graded Lie algebra over K. The grading identifies a Jordan algebra for each simple root αj of R, with the following restrictions given by the Dynkin type of the simple factor containing αj :

Ap: We identify p copies of an associative Jordan algebra, one for each simple root. When p = 2 we could instead identify two copies of an octonion algebra over K′, where K′

is a finite field extension of K.When p = 1 , we can additionally identify any semisimple Jordan algebra over K.

Bp: We identify p − 1 copies of K′ and one copy of a Clifford type algebra over K′.

Cp: We identify p − 1 copies of J(R) associated to each short root and one copy of H(R, ⋆ ) to the unique long root, for R a finite-dimensional algebra over K′.When p = 3 , we could instead identify two copies of an octonion algebra over K′ for each short root and a copy of K′ for the long root. 

F4: We identify 2 copies of K′ associated to the long roots and 2 copies of C, a Cayley-Dickson algebra over K′ associated to the short roots. 

G2: We identify one copy of K′ for the long root. The short root either identifies a copy of 

H3(C) or a semisimple Jordan algebra consisting of K′ plus a Clifford type algebra. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 31 

Dp: We identify p copies of K′, one for each simple root. 

E6, E7, E8: We identify a copy of K′ for each simple root. Proof. The key idea is that the Dynkin diagrams which occur in the Satake-Tits index are in the same category of diagram as the original restricted root system. So the classification of the Jordan algebra associated to each node can be read from either the restricted root system or the Satake-Tits index. For example consider the case Ap. By Lemma 3.34, the subset ∆◦ ⊂ ∆alg is Jordan compatible with an associated Ap grading. There are only two possible choices for this: 

An(p, k ) and E∗ 

> 6

Remark 3.36 . Regardless of Dynkin-type, the Jordan algebra for K′, a finite field extension of 

K can be identified for every simple root. For example, in type Ap, K′ is an associative Jordan algebra or in type Bp, K′ is a one dimensional Clifford algebra over K′.This identification theorem is equivalent to the recognition theorems for simple graded Lie algebras. It is interesting to compare the identification theorem with the recognition theorem in type C2/B 2. In the identification theorem, we separated the Lie algebras into two families 

B2 : K′ ⇐ J(VK′ , b ) and C2 : J(R) ⇒ H(R, ⋆ ).

When R⋆ ≃ K′ is commutative, these families agree as x 7 → xx ⋆ defines a quadratic form on R

realizing J(R) as a Clifford type Jordan algebra. However when R⋆ is noncommutative these families are distinct. This helps to explain the complications in the C2-recognition theorem (Theorem 3.15). 3.8. Noncommutative Lie algebras over Jordan algebras. In the previous sections, we have started with a simple Lie algebra, g, and classified the possible root systems R which can grade g. This classification identified an isotopy class of Jordan algebras for each node of the Dynkin diagram for R and gave restrictions on the types of Jordan algebras that appear. We now reverse the process and begin with a simple root system R and a compatible family of Jordan algebras {Ji}. Each Jordan algebra determines a Satake-Tits index of type A1 which can be glued to obtain the Satake-Tits index of an R-graded Lie algebra, g, which identifies the chosen Ji.32 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Definition 3.37. We write T (R, {Ji}) for the (adjoint form) of Lie algebra with R-grading and the collection {Ji} of Jordan algebras assigned to its nodes. If g is a finite central extension of 

T (R, {Ji}), we call it a Jordan split algebra of type R over the Jordan algebras {Ji}.

T (R, {Ji}) is a unification of the Tits construction and the Tits-Kantor-Koecher construction which spells out the dependence on the Jordan algebras associated to each root space. 

Remark 3.38 . Gradings by folding correspond to using non-simple Jordan algebras in T (R, {Ji}).For example, the F4 grading of e(6) 6 is given by T (F4, {Ji}) with J1 = J2 = R and J3 = J4 = Cspl .Similarly there is a G2-grading of so (p + 3 , p + 4 + q) given by T (G2, {Ji}) with long root space J1 = R, and short root space R ⊕ J(p, p + 1 + q). This folds the B3-grading given by 

T (B3, {R, R, J(p, p + 1 + q)}).

Definition 3.39. Let g be Jordan split of type R, so that g ≃ T (R, {Ji}). The number of noncommutative Jordan algebras in {Ji} is the noncommutative rank or NC Rank of g

with respect to R.By the identification theorem Theorem 3.35, there are at most two different isotopy classes of Jordan algebras in the collection {Ji}. Each of these isotopy classes is assigned to a short or long root. 

Proposition 3.40. We have the following classification of simple Jordan split g of type R by noncommutative rank: (1) NC Rank = 0 : g is split or quasi-split and R is the root system of g.(2) NC Rank = 1 : R is type A1, Bp, or G2 and not split or quasi-split. (3) NC Rank > 1: R is type Ap, Cp, with p > 1, or F4 and not split or quasi-split. Proof. First, if g is split or quasi-split, then by definition there is a Borel subalgebra in g, this means that the structure groups of all of the Jordan algebras appearing when we choose Θ = ∆ 

are abelian, which means they are commutative Jordan algebras. For the second statement, we look at the conditions for Bp and G2 and see that in either case a commutative Jordan algebra must be assigned to the long roots of the root system. Since there is only one short root remaining in each type, the non split or quasi-split type A1, Bp,and G2 have noncommutative rank 1. There is a slight ambiguity since B2 ≃ C2, but we will consider NC rank 1 groups of this type as B2 and higher NC rank groups as C2.For the last part we only have to consider the Cp case and see that the Hermitian type algebra is the fixed points of an involution on the associative type algebra, meaning that if the associative algebra is commutative, then all are and we are back in the quasi-split case. Since 

p > 2 this implies that the NC rank is bigger than 1. Every possible simple root system is covered by these cases, so the classification is complete. 

□

Example 3.41 . Over the real numbers, the noncommutative rank 0 Jordan split Lie algebras are the split real Lie algebras, the complex Lie algebras viewed as real Lie algebras and the real forms su (n, n ), so (n, n + 2) and e(2) 6 .3.9. Weyl groups. When g is graded by a root system R there are two Weyl groups that act on G, the Weyl group of R, W (R), and the Weyl group of the restricted root system of g. Let 

∆ be a choice of simple restricted roots for g and let W (∆) be the Weyl group of the restricted root system. In this section we define two injections W (R) → W (∆) allowing us to understand 

W (R) using W (∆) .

Convention. We use s1, . . . , s n for the generators of W (∆) , and ς1 . . . , ς p for those of W (R).

Definition 3.42. For w ∈ W (∆) we denote by ℓ(w) the length of w in the generators si.For F ⊂ ∆, any subset of simple roots, we denote by wF the longest element of the Weyl group W (F ) < W (∆) generated by F . We will denote by w0 the longest element of W (R).NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 33

Finally we return to the case of arbitrary R-gradings. By Proposition 3.30, at worst the associated parabolic pΘ induces an R′-grading on g with R′ folding onto R. The simple roots of 

R′ that are grouped by the folding have commuting reflections and so we have a natural injective map W (R) → W (R′) sending ςRβ to Q ςRαi. Post composing with the injections for R′ define the required injections for any grading. 4. Jordan split groups 

We now focus on the structure of algebraic groups with Lie algebra graded by a root system 

R. We identify a “good” set of generators to understand G via the root system R in analogy to a choice of Chevalley basis of a split group. These generators have simple relations which can be used to lift the Weyl group of R to G and compute the Gauss decomposition of products of elements. Finally we generalize the fundamental weights of a root system to functions which take values in the Jordan algebras. These Jordan weights will provide the coordinate functions needed to define cluster structures in Part 3. 

Definition 4.1. An algebraic group G is Jordan split of type R if its Lie algebra is (a central extension of) T (R, {Ji}) for some collection of Jordan algebras {Ji}.Since Lie algebras can be graded by root systems of several types, the same group G can be Jordan split in several ways. 

Example 4.2 .

(1) The group SL 6(R) is Jordan split in three ways. Its Lie algebra sl 6(R) is isomorphic to 

T (A5, {R, R, R, R, R}), T (A3, {M2(R), M2(R), M2(R)}), and T (A2, {M3(R), M3(R)}).(2) Also Sp 6(R) is Jordan split in two ways, sp 6(R) ≃ T (C3, {R, R, R}) ≃ T (A1, {H3(R)}).(3) For a final example consider SO(3 , 8) . It is Jordan split in three ways as well, so (3 , 8) = 

T (B3, {R, R, J(0 , 5) }) ≃ T (B2, {R, J(1 , 6) }) ≃ T (A1, {J(2 , 7) }).Given a Jordan split group G of type R, our key construction is what we call the Jordan pinning of the group. As motivation, we first look at groups with an A1-graded Lie algebra. 4.1. A1-graded groups. The recognition theorem for A1 allows us to reduce the study of 

A1-graded Lie algebras to the Lie algebras sl 2(J) for some Jordan algebra J = ( V, ι, 1).
