In the following sections, we will generalize everything above to a Jordan split group over an arbitrary simple root system. 4.2. Jordan pinning. In this section, we define a Jordan pinning for a algebraic group, which plays a key role in the remainder of the paper. We will use it to understand generic elements of the group and eventually to define coordinates on the associated flag varieties and local systems. 

Definition 4.17. Let G be a Jordan split group over a root system R indexed by simple roots 

Θ = {β1, · · · βp}. Let Ji be a representative in the isotopy class of Jordan algebras such that 

g = T (R, {Ji}). A Jordan pinning of G is a collection of homomorphisms ˇβi : Γ( Ji) → G

and maps xi, y i : Vi → G indexed by i ∈ Θ such that for i ∈ Θ the maps xi, y i, ˇβi form a homomorphism φi : SL 2(Ji) → G by 

φi (A) = ˇβ(A), φi

 exp( v(1) ) = xi(v), φi

 exp( v(−1) 

) = yi(v) .

We call the Jordan pinning normalized if it furthermore satisfies that for each βi ∈ Θ we have 

xi(1i) ∈ exp( gδ1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ gδci ) and yi(1i) ∈ exp( g−δ1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ g−δci ) .

If a Jordan pinning is not normalized we can always change Ji by an isotopy to make it so. This does not affect the homomorphisms from SL 2(Ji), so we will always take normalized Jordan pinnings. 

Definition 4.18. The Levi factor , LΘ, of a Jordan pinning of G of type R is the subgroup generated by { ˇβi(A)|A ∈ Γ( Ji), i ∈ Θ}. This is also the Levi subgroup of the associated parabolic subgroup, PΘ, of the grading (Definition 3.10). NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 41 

Definition 4.19. The Jordan pinning induces extended root maps βi : LΘ → PΓ( Ji) from the Levi factor to the adjoint structure group for each i by lx i(v)l−1 = xi (βi(l)( v)) . These extend the usual root maps from Z(LΘ) → K× given by the inclusion of the split group of type 

R into G.

Remark 4.20 . LΘ acts on the set of Jordan pinnings by conjugation. We can view this action as changing each Ji by an isomorphism. Therefore, the action of LΘ on the space of pinnings identifies a twisted form for each Ji. We expect that the set of pinnings with respect to a given set of twisted forms is an LΘ-torsor as in the classic pinnings of a split group. Suppose that βi and βj are connected in R and Ji is one dimensional (and hence isomorphic to K). Now fix a Jordan pinning of G. The composition βi ◦ ˇβj is a character of Γ( Jj ). We can use this to define a map N : V ×

4.3. Jordan weights. Now we assume that G is such that G is simply connected with respect to a root system R. We wish to define a collection of functions dual to the functions ˇβi to generalize the fundamental weights of split algebraic group. In noncommutative rank less than or equal to one, what we also call lower noncommutative rank, there is a natural system generalizing the fundamental weights. However in noncommutative rank bigger or equal to two, what we also call higher noncommutative rank, we must weaken the duality between coroot and fundamental weights. With this weakened duality there will be many possible systems of weights and we will prove our constructions are independent of the choice. 

Definition 4.27. A system of Jordan weights is a set of functions Λi : LΘ → Γ( Ji) such that: (1) Λi( ˇβj (Λ j (l))) = 

(

1 i̸ = j

Λj (l) i = j. (weak duality) (2) For all i̸ = j, ˇβi(Λ i(l)) ˇβj (Λ j (l)) = ˇβj (Λ j (l)) ˇβi(Λ i(l)) . (commutativity) (3) For all l ∈ LΘ, Q 

> i

ˇβi(Λ i(l)) = l. (reconstructability) We say a system is faithful on βi if Λi( ˇβi(a)) = a.We note that the Jordan algebras, {Ji} are only well-defined up to isotopy. In every case it is possible to choose the isotopy class so that for any i, j one of Ji or Jj is a Jordan subalgebra of the other. This implies that we can choose a pining which identifies a single identity element for all the Jordan algebras and we can consider the target of Λi to be the same for all i.

Example 4.28 . When pinning SL 6(R) with Θ = {α2, α 4}, both J1 and J2 are isotopic to M2(R).We will take the identity matrix as the identity element in both cases. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 43 

Similarly when pinning Sp 12 (R) with Θ = {α2, α 4, α 6} we have J1 and J2 isotopic to M2(R)

and J3 isotopic to H2(R). Choosing the identity matrix as the identity in all three cases realizes the required subalgebra conditions. 

Remark 4.29 . The Jordan weights naturally extend to Gauss decomposable elements of G, by applying the weight to the Levi factor [g]0.

Proposition 4.30. In noncommutative rank 0 or 1, there is a unique system of Jordan weights that is faithful on all simple roots. Proof. In rank 0, the usual fundamental weights provide such a system. In rank 1, property (3) determines Λp by forcing Λp

  ˇβp(A) = A and all other Λi to be fundamental weights. □

We now focus on higher noncommutative rank. As a motivating example, consider the group 

SL 6(C) the A2-pinning analogous to Example 4.24. The Levi subgroup is identified with the triple of matrices (A, B, C ) with det( ABC ) = 1 . So any element of the Levi can be decomposed as ˇβ1(A, A −1) ˇβ2(AB, C ). The natural guess of Jordan weights is Λ1(A, B, C ) = ( A, A −1) and 

Λ2(A, B, C ) = ( AB, C ) which has weak duality and reconstructability but not commutativity. Instead there are two choices of weights which are faithful either on ˇβ1 or ˇβ2 but not both: 

Λ1(A, B, C ) = ( A, pdet( AB )−1

B) Λ2(A, B, C ) = ( pdet( AB )12, C ) or 

Λ1(A, B, C ) = ( A, pdet( A)−1

12) Λ2(A, B, C ) = ( pdet( A)B, C ) .

Over non algebraically closed fields, the square roots we used might not be well defined. Instead we use a weak system of Jordan weights which lands in a central extension of the structure group by K. The coroot maps can be similarly extended to map the extended structure group into a central extension of G. Under this construction the weak equivalent of the first set of weights above is 

Λ1(A, B, C ) = ( A, B ) Λ2(A, B, C ) = ( 12, C ) .

The difference between weak and standard systems of Jordan weights is only important for some groups of higher noncommutative rank. As such, we will drop the word weak for the rest of the paper. 

Proposition 4.32. In higher noncommutative rank there exists many (weak) systems of Jordan weights. Moreover, given a root βi we can choose a system that is faithful on βi.Proof. There are two families of root systems that admit higher noncommutative rank gradings, 

Ap, C p and three exceptional cases A2, C3, and F4.We begin with type Ap. Let R be a finite dimensional algebra over K and consider the group 

G := GL p+1 (R). Let PΘ be the parabolic subgroup consisting of upper triangular matrices with Levi subgroup GL 1(R)p+1 . This parabolic subgroup induces an Ap-grading on SL p+1 (R) where each Jordan algebra is J(R).There is a natural Jordan pinning of G given by the p standard embeddings of SL 2(R) → G

as 2 × 2 diagonal blocks. To define a weak system, we extend the coroot maps ˇβi to have domain 

GL 1(R) × GL 1(R), so that these extended pinning maps are maps from GL 2(R) → G. This identifies LΘ with GL 1(R)p+1 with the image of ˇβi contained in the factors i and i + 1 .We can now construct a (weak) system of Jordan weights faithful on βi by defining the image of each SL 1(R) factor to either be the trivial map to 1 or the identity map. Each Λj will map factors other than j or j + 1 using the trivial map. To be faithful, we define Λi to be the identity map on both factors i and i + 1 . We then define for j < i , Λj the trivial map on factor j + 1 

and the identity map on factor j. Similarly for j > i we take Λj trivial on factor j and the identity on factor j + 1 . This construction maps each factor with identity map exactly once, so it satisfies commutativity and reconstructability. 44 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD

for i < p and xHi (v) = xGi (v) for i = p. A faithful choice of Jordan weights is given by a symmetric choice of faithful weights on GL 2p(R).Finally, consider the exceptional groups of higher noncommutative rank over A2, C 3, or F4.In every case the absolute root system is type F4, E6, E7 or E8. Over an algebraically close field, their Levi subgroups are central extensions of Spin(8) or (SL 2)3, while the structure groups are central extensions of Spin(8) and (SL 2)2 respectively. The weight functions are then constructed analogously by choosing isomorphisms on the factors corresponding to βi for Λi and the identity map on those factors for overlapping weights. In all cases we see that there is a choice of Jordan weights faithful on the given noncommu-tative root βi. □

4.4. Lifts of the grading Weyl group. The Jordan pinning over the root system R will allow us to lift the Weyl group W (R) of R to G. This lift will act on the decomposition of G into 

R-root spaces as the ordinary Weyl group acts on the split algebraic group associated to R. Let 

∆R be the Dynkin diagram for the root system R.

ςi := xi(−1i)yi(1i)xi(−1i) and ςi := yi(−1i)xi(1i)yi(−1i) .

4.6. Root system calculus. The above results enable us to make calculations in Jordan split groups of type R that are independent of the choice of Jordan algebras. The calculations depend only on the root system, and we call this the root system calculus .Let G be a Jordan split group of type R and fix a normalized Jordan pinning of G. This defines elements xi(v), y i(v), ˇβi(a) for v ∈ Ji and a ∈ Γ( Ji). In Section 4.4, we also defined elements ςi and ςi which lift the Weyl group of type R to G. The following lemmas provide all relations among these generators needed for the remainder of the paper. Many of the lemmas in this section are restatements of earlier lemmas gathered here for easy reference. We begin with relations that are entirely within a single pinned SL 2(Ji).

Next we consider the relations among pinning maps from different roots. 

Lemma 4.45. Let i̸ = j index two simple roots in R and consider their connection in the Dynkin diagram. 

• Regardless of connection, xi(v)yj (w) = yj (w)xi(v).

• If i is disconnected from j then xi(v)xj (w) = xj (w)xi(v) and yi(v)yj (w) = yj (w)yi(v).Proof. The first and second statement follow from computing the brackets of the corresponding root spaces in g. The brackets vanish as βi − βj is never a root and βi + βj isn’t a root when i is not connected to j in a simple Dynkin diagram. So by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff theorem the corresponding group elements commute. □

# Part 2. Noncommutative Cluster Varieties 

Our next goal is to develop a theory of noncommutative cluster varieties rich enough to provide cluster structures for the natural geometric objects related to Jordan split groups. In noncommutative rank 0, the algebraic groups are quasi-split and the classical cluster structure will suffice as described in [GS19]. In our previous work, we defined a noncommutative cluster framework that suffices for noncommutative rank 1 [GKNW26]. In higher noncommutative rank, we instead require a further generalization of the noncommutative wiring diagrams defined in [GK24]. In this part, we review the classical cluster theory and describe the two noncommutative generalizations needed for the remainder of the paper. 5. Classic cluster algebras 

We first recall the construction of a cluster algebra. It is grown from an initial seed which consists of a quiver and a set of variables associated to each node of the quiver. 5.1. Quivers and seed mutation. Definition 5.1. A quiver Q is a directed graph on n nodes with no self loops or two cycles. The nodes of Q are partitioned into two sets, the mutable/unfrozen nodes and frozen nodes. A quiver with weighted nodes is a quiver where each node is assigned a positive integer weight dk. The adjacency matrix of a weighted quiver is a skew-symmetrizable matrix ϵij 

with entries ϵij = eij dj / gcd( dj , d i), where eij is the number of arrows from node i to node j

counted with signs. Note that the diagonal matrix D with Dii = di is the skew symmetrizer of ϵ, in other words 

Dϵ is skew symmetric. 

Example 5.2 . We will only need quivers with nodes of two different weights. These will either be in {1, 2} or in {1, 3}. In either case we draw nodes of higher weight larger than nodes of weight 1. For example in Figure 6 the first two nodes are weight 1 while the rightmost node is weight 2. The skew-symmetrizable adjacency matrix is also given. −1 0 30 −1 0

 .

It will always be clear from the context which case we are dealing with. 

Definition 5.3. A seed , (Q, a) consists of a weighted quiver Q and a list of algebraically independent variables a = ( a1, . . . , a n) in K(x1, . . . , x n) associated to the n nodes of Q. The variables ai are called cluster variables .Given a seed (Q, a) and a mutable node k, we can produce a new seed μk(Q, a) = ( Q′, a′) by a process called mutation which consists of mutating the quiver Q and the cluster variables. 

Definition 5.4. The mutation of Q at node k is the quiver μk(Q) formed by (1) For each pair of arrows i → k → j add dk gcd( di, d j )/(gcd( di, d k) gcd( dk, d j )) arrows from i to j.(2) Remove any 2-cycles created by the previous step. (3) Reverse all arrows incident to k.This procedure is equivalent to the following operation on the adjacency matrix: (4) b′

Cluster algebras are of particular interest because they endow the coordinate rings of some varieties with a combinatorial structure: We say a variety has a cluster structure if there is a seed which generates a cluster algebras isomorphic to the coordinate ring of the variety. Thus we will often implicitly identify the abstract cluster coordinates with elements of the given ring. 

Example 5.8 . Consider the decorated flag variety A for SL 2(R). It consists of nonzero vectors in R2. The configuration space 

Conf 4(A ) = SL 2(R)\A 4

consists of 4-tuples of decorated flags up to the diagonal action of SL 2(R). The subspace

(A ) where no two vectors lie on a common line has a cluster structure given by ei-ther quiver in Figure 7. Namely, if the configuration x is represented by (v1, . . . , v 4), we can compute coordinate functions 

aij (x) := det( vi|vj )

which are clearly invariant under the action of SL 2(R) on A 4 and thus descend to Conf ×

which has exactly the form of the exchange relation Equation (5). Thus, we can assign the coordinate functions to the nodes of the quivers in Figure 7 as a = a14 , b = a12 , c = a32 , d =

a43 , e = a13 and f = a42 .This is the most basic example of the theory we will discuss in Part 3. 5.2. Cluster algebras from surfaces. The quivers in Figure 7 have another interesting prop-erty: They are obtained from the two triangulations of the square by drawing a node for every arc in the triangulation (where the boundary arcs correspond to frozen nodes) and inscribing a clockwise three-cycle of arrows into every triangle. Cluster algebras arising in this way were studied in [FG07, FST08]. For any quiver obtained from a triangulated surface in the way described above, any single mutation is equivalent to a flip in the triangulation. This is the operation where one diagonal of a square is removed and replaced with the other one. The exchange relation for such a mutation can be read off in an interesting way: Consider a square as in Figure 8. To replace the diagonal with the other one, we consider the two zigzag paths which connect the endpoints of the new diagonal. 

abcde

Figure 8. Mutation and zigzag paths. By writing down the cluster variables we encounter along these two paths we get the expression (6) aec + bed = f e 2

where f is the new cluster variable obtained by mutation and the equality simply follows by multiplying the exchange relation by e. In this commutative setting neither the orientation of the paths nor the order of the product matter. But we observe the surface can provide a natural order which is exactly the approach used by Berenstein-Retakh in their definition of noncommutative surfaces [BR18]. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 51 

5.3. Key properties. We now recall a few key properties of cluster algebras [FZ02]. 

Proposition 5.9. If two seeds (Q, a) and (Q′, a′) are related by a sequence of mutations, they produce isomorphic cluster algebras. 

Proposition 5.10 (Laurent Phenomenon) . Every cluster variable can be written as a Laurent polynomial in the cluster variables from a single seed (Q, a). Moreover the coefficients of the Laurent polynomial are positive. 

Definition 5.11. Given a (semi)ring R, the R-points of a cluster algebra are the homomor-phisms from the cluster algebra to R. When R = R this is equivalent to choosing a real number for each cluster variable subject to the exchange relations. An R-point is called positive if all the cluster variables are evaluated at strictly positive real numbers. 

Proposition 5.12 (Positivity) . The positive R-points of a cluster algebra can be constructed by evaluating the cluster variables of any seed at positive real numbers and using the exchange relations to evaluate all other cluster variables. 

Corollary 5.13. The positive points of a cluster algebra form a well-defined semi-algebraic set. Furthermore a positive point can be specified by choosing arbitrary positive numbers for all the cluster variables in a single seed (Q, a).

6. Polygonal cluster algebras 

We now recall some of the basic constructions from [GKNW26] we will need to describe the cluster structure on our noncommutative cluster coordinates. We introduce a small modification to include the G2 case. In this section we fix a parameter r which will be 2 in type Bp and 3 in type G2.6.1. 2-colored and ordered quivers. All of the quivers we will consider are weighted quivers where the weights are either 1 or r. We denote by Q(1) 0 and Q(2) 0 the set of weight one and weight r nodes respectively. We call the weight 1 nodes commutative nodes and the weight 

r nodes noncommutative nodes .

Definition 6.1. A 2-colored quiver Q is a weighted quiver where all of the arrows between noncommutative nodes are partitioned into two sets called parallel and crossing arrows re-spectively. The combination of one parallel and one crossing arrow with the same source and target is called a paired arrow .The pruned quiver denoted by Qpr is obtained by removing all of the commutative nodes and all paired arrows between weight r nodes. We call the arrows in Qpr noncommutative arrows .

Convention. We draw parallel arrows by black arrows with solid tips ( ) and crossing arrows by blue arrows with a hollow tip ( _). Paired arrows are green with a double arrow head ( ↠). Crossing arrows are assigned weight r −1, so that during mutation they count as r −1 arrows. Consequently, paired arrows have weight r. This is reflected in the following mutation rule: 

Definition 6.2. Mutation of a 2-colored quiver is defined by the following rules: First suppose that k ∈ Q(2) 0 is a weight r node, then mutation at k is determined by the following process: (1) For every sequence i → k → j with i, j weight r nodes, introduce arrows i → j, whose colors are determined by the coloring rules 

+ =

_ + _ = + ( r − 2) ↠

_ + = _ .

If instead at least one of i, j is weight one, then the new arrows are always colored black. (2) Cancel any resulting 2-cycles of the same color. Note that a paired arrow can cancel with either a parallel or a crossing arrow to result in an arrow of the opposite class: 

+ ↞ = ^_ + ↞ =52 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

(3) Reverse any arrow connected to k.If instead k ∈ Q(1) 0 is weight one then mutation is determined as follows: (1) For every sequence i → k → j with i, j weight r nodes, introduce a paired arrow i↠j. If instead at least one of i, j is weight one, then the new arrows are always colored black. (2) Cancel any resulting 2-cycles of the same color. (3) Reverse arrows connected to k.

Remark 6.3 . The key motivation for these definitions comes from the type of coordinates we will define on Θ-positive representations. In these cases, we assign elements of the structure group Γ( J) of a Jordan algebra to the non commutative nodes of Q and real numbers to the commutative nodes. The number r is the degree of the norm map of J. The commutative arrows will really ‘see’ the norm of the elements while crossing arrows will ‘see’ an involution τ applied to the corresponding elements. 

Example 6.4 . Some examples of 2-colored quivers and their mutation are shown in Figure 9.

Figure 9. 2-colored quivers and their mutation. Adding color to the quivers is not sufficient to capture the combinatorics of the coordinates which we will discuss in Part 3. For this, we will also need to take the order into account since some of the functions will take values in a non-abelian group. This requires a further upgrade of the quivers: 

Definition 6.5. An ordered (2-colored) quiver consists of a 2-colored quiver Q together with a splitting of the incoming and outgoing arrows in the pruned quiver Qpr at every node 

k ∈ Q(2) 0 into two ordered tuples, denoted In f (k) and Out f (k) for f ∈ { 0, 1}.

In •(k)Out •(k)Out ◦(k)In ◦(k)

k

Out c(k)

Figure 10. Decoration of big nodes in an ordered quiver with partition of the arrows. 

Convention. We draw the noncommutative nodes of an ordered quiver as shown in Figure 10. The order of the tuples is given by going counterclockwise around the node. We call the two halves open and filled, respectively. To match the pictures we use ◦ for 0 and • for 1. The commutative arrows (arrows connected to weight 1 nodes and paired arrows) connected to a node k ∈ Q(2) 0 are denoted by the sets In c(k) and Out c(k).In order to mutate such quivers we restrict to a special subclass. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 53 

6.2. Decorated polygons. In [GKNW26] we introduced the notion of source-target-compatible quivers using an algebraic compatibility condition, and proved that these are in correspondence with decorated polygonal tiling. To keep the exposition concise, we will simply consider deco-rated polygons and their corresponding quivers here. 

Definition 6.6. A decorated polygon consists of the following data: 

• a polygon Pn,

• an orientation of its sides, 

• a partition of its vertices into two disjoint sets, called parallel and crossing angles 

such that there is an odd number of parallel angles ,

• a choice of vertex for every side such that the straight lines connecting the midpoint of each side to its corresponding vertex intersect pairwise (allowing intersection at the endpoints). We call these lines angle indicators .Some examples of decorated polygons are visualized in Figure 11. We indicate a parallel angle by a black solid arc near the vertex, while a crossing angle is indicated by a blue dashed arc. 

Figure 11. Examples of decorated polygons. Given a decorated polygon we construct an ordered quiver as follows: (1) For every side, we get a noncommutative node such that the filled half is on the left as given by the orientation. (2) The halves of the nodes facing into the polygon are connected with arrows: For a given node k all nodes corresponding to sides to the right of the angle indicator are sources of arrows with target k, while all nodes which correspond to sides to the left of the angle indicator are targets of arrows starting at k.(3) The order of the arrows at every node is given by the clockwise order of the sides. (4) To determine the color of the arrows, start by coloring the arrows between nodes cor-responding to adjacent sides: Unless all angle indicators end at the vertex between the two sides, color the arrow the color of the angle. Otherwise take the opposite color. Color all remaining arrows such that every set of three nodes 

• has an odd number of parallel arrows if the nodes form a 3-cycle, 

• has an even number of parallel arrows otherwise. 

Remark 6.7 . The coloring rules determine a unique coloring of the quiver [GKNW26]. The ordered quivers associated to the polygons in Figure 11 are shown in Figure 12. 

Figure 12. Ordered quivers associated to the decorated polygons in Figure 11. By gluing decorated polygons along their sides, we can use them to tile a surface. 

Definition 6.8. A decorated tiling of an oriented surface S with marked points consists of the following data 54 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

• a set of non-intersecting oriented arcs between marked points on S, such that the com-plementary regions are polygons, 

• an admissible decoration for every such polygon such that the orientation of the edges agree with the orientation of the arcs on the surface, Using the same rules as above for every polygon in the tiling, we obtain an ordered quiver from every decorated tiling. 

Definition 6.9. An ordered quiver for which the pruned quiver can be represented as a deco-rated tiling is called a polygonal quiver .

Remark 6.10 . This differs slightly from the terminology in [GKNW26]. There we gave an alternate classification of these quivers which we called ‘source-target compatible’. We can now use the polygons to mutate polygonal quivers: 

Definition 6.11. Let Q be an polygonal quiver corresponding to a decorated tiling T . The (admissible) mutation at a node k produces a new polygonal quiver μk(Q) as follows: (1) Perform a mutation of the underlying 2-colored quiver at k.(2) Order the arrows at every node such that the ordered quiver corresponds to a decorated tiling T ′. The tiling T ′ is determined by the following conditions: 

• If k ∈ Q(1) 0 , then T ′ has the same polygons but possibly different angle indicators. 

• If k ∈ Q(2) 0 , then T ′ is related to T by a flip as shown in Figure 13. All the other angle indicators and angle colors are determined by the mutated 2-colored quiver. We denote by |Q| the set of polygonal quivers which are obtained from Q by sequences of admissible mutations. 

Figure 13. A flip in a decorated tiling. 

Remark 6.12 . The second step is not always possible: There are polygonal quivers for which a mutation of the underlying 2-colored quiver does not lead to a quiver which can be obtained from the prescribed tiling. We do not consider mutations at such nodes. 

Example 6.13 . Let us illustrate the mutation procedure and possible inadmissibility of mutations with two examples: Figure 14 illustrates an admissible mutation at a noncommutative node. The order of the arrows is deduced from the decorated tilings which are also shown On the other hand, consider the polygonal quiver in Figure 15 with its pruned part represented by the decorated triangle as shown. Mutation at the commutative node is not admissible because the new 2-colored quiver cannot be represented by changing the angle indicators of the decorated polygon. Finally, we define two operations on polygonal quivers which will be used later: 

Definition 6.14. Let Q be an polygonal quiver with decorated tiling T , k a noncommutative node of Q, respectively a side in T . The switch at k gives an polygonal quiver σk(Q) which corresponds to the tiling σk(T ) where the orientation of k is reversed. When r = 2 , we moreover define: The weaving at k gives wk(Q) corresponding to wk(T )

where the types of all angles adjacent to k is switched, i.e. straight angles become crossing and vice versa. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 55 

The first operation corresponds to reversing the fill of the node k, while the second operation switches the color of all arrows connected to k. Since we would like these operations to commute with mutation, the weaving only makes sense if r = 2 because for r = 3 the different kinds of arrows behave combinatorially quite differently. 6.3. Definition 6.16. Let γ be an angle path in a tiling P . The label L(γ) ∈ FQ of γ is computed recursively according to the following rules: 

• The label of a path following an angle is 1. 

• The label along a side i is στ (Xi) if the path follows the orientation, τ (Xi) otherwise.

These maps define weaving and switch maps of the associated seed tori. 6.4. Jordan algebra points. So far we have given a description of the mutation of polygonal quivers, and we have assigned a group QQ and a torus AQ to each one. When r = 2 it is possible to define a polygonal cluster algebra given by gluing together quotients of a quiver algebra 

[GKNW26]. The defining relations were constructed so that the algebra could be evaluated in arbitrary Clifford algebras. However for r = 3 , such an algebra does not exist. Nevertheless, we can define “evaluations” of 

QQ into arbitrary Jordan algebras. These evaluations can be glued together to form a geometric object in analogy with the construction of a cluster variety for classic commutative cluster algebras. Now let J = ( V, ι, 1) be a normed Jordan algebra of degree r.

Definition 6.21. Let Q be an polygonal quiver. A J-point of the seed tori AQ is a group homomorphism ϕ : QQ → Γ( J) such that: (1) ϕ intertwines the σ and τ defined for QQ with those defined for Γ( J).(2) ϕ(∆) = ι(v∆) for some v∆ ∈ V for any angle ∆.We denote by AQ(J) the set of J-points of AQ.

Remark 6.22 . Because of the first requirement, we can define a J-point of AQ by picking images 

ϕ(Xi) for i ∈ Q(2) 0 and ϕ(Nj ) for j ∈ Q(1) 0 . This determines the images of all other generating variables. Also, observe that ϕ(Ni) ∈ K× for any i ∈ Q0.For the second condition, it suffices to check one angle per polygon. This is because angles in the same polygon with different base points are related by applying στ and ∆ 7 → A∆σ(A) for 

A ∈ Γ( J). Both of these operations preserve ι(V ) ⊂ Γ( J).If J moreover has a positive structure, we can define positive points: 

Definition 6.23. We say that a J-point ϕ : QQ → J is positive if (1) ϕ(Xi) ∈ Γ( J)◦ for all i ∈ Q(2) 0 .(2) ϕ(Ni) ∈ K>0 for all i ∈ Q(1) 0 .(3) v∆ ∈ V >0 for any angle ∆.The set of such points is denoted AQ(J>0).58 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Remark 6.24 . Again, it is sufficient to check condition (3) for one angle per polygon as the operations in Remark 6.22 preserve the cone if we restrict to the identity component of the structure group, which is condition (1). Our next goal is to define a mutation of Jordan points, i.e. a map AQ(J) → A Q′ (J) that is defined whenever Q′ is obtained from Q by (admissible) mutation. Note that this map will only be defined for an open subset of Jordan points. This gives rise to a birational map of seed tori 

AQ → A Q′ .

Remark 6.26 . Note that in the mutation map, the only elements which are added are in the vector space V (which gets mapped to Γ( J) via ι), and the only elements which are multiplied belong to Γ( J). As such J does not need to be contained in an algebra. If we consider a positive J-point of QQ, the new angle always lies in ι(V ×):

Lemma 6.27. An admissible mutation μk : Q → Q′ sends positive J-points to positive J-points. Proof. Consider a positive J-point of AQ. Then, in particular, the elements v∆lf (k) and v∆rf (k)

from the angle summation formulas are in V >0. This positive cone is preserved by the action of elements in Γ( J)◦ and R>0 and addition. Thus the new angles given by Definition 6.25 are again in V >0. As all other angles are unchanged or equivalent to the new angles by actions of elements in Γ( J)◦ the resulting point is also positive. □

Definition 6.28. We write A|Q| for the noncommutative space which is obtained by gluing together the seed tori along all sequences of admissible mutation maps. Given a normed Jordan algebra of degree r J we write A|Q|(J) for the set of Jordan points of A|Q|.NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 59 

The previous lemma implies that the set of positive Jordan points A|Q|(J>0) can be computed on any single seed torus, as the mutation maps are isomorphisms on the positive points. In [GKNW26] for the case r = 2 , we construct an analog of the cluster algebra called the 

noncommutative polygonal cluster algebra which contains all of the cluster variables ob-tained by admissible mutation and prove a noncommutative Laurent phenomenon. This implies that the ring of global functions on A|Q| contains the noncommutative polygonal cluster algebra associated to Q.

Remark 6.30 . Deducing Equation (9) from Equation (8) requires a slightly more careful analysis of the powers of στ . This uses the parity condition for decorated polygons and can be found in [GKNW26]. 7. Noncommutative networks 

In higher noncommutative rank, we use different combinatorial data to provide coordinates, a planar bipartite network. These networks were introduced by Postnikov to study the Grass-mannian [Pos06] and have many applications to cluster algebra theory [FWZ21]. The noncom-mutative generalization we consider here is mostly the same as that of [GK24]. 

Definition 7.1. A plabic network on a disk is a bipartite graph embedded in a disk with edge weights in an (associative) algebra R such that (1) A vertex is degree 1 if and only if it is on the boundary of the disk. (2) The graph in the interior of the disk is planar. (3) The counterclockwise product of edge weights around any vertex is 1.Plabic networks are considered up to natural equivalences given by isotopy of the disk fixing the boundary and contracting/adding vertices of degree 2 (Figure 19). 

A A−1

⇐⇒ 

A A−1

⇐⇒ 

Figure 19. Local equivalence moves of plabic networks. We will be interested in control over the boundary edges which cause the network to be nonplanar. 

Definition 7.2. A triangular grounded wiring network on n strands is a bipartite network embedded in a triangle with a designated ground side such that (1) The left and right sides of the triangle have n • vertices labeled 1 to n.(2) The edges incident to the ground are called legs . Removing all the legs results in a planar graph. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 61 

(3) The non leg edges can be partitioned into two groups, strands and bridges such that the side boundary nodes labeled i are connected by a unique path of strands. A general grounded wiring network on a disk is a network embedded in disk with a trian-gulation of the disk such that the network restricted to each triangle is a triangular grounded wiring network. A grounded wiring network can be obtained by amalgamating copies of the elementary net-works in Figure 20 along the boundary vertices with matching strand numbers and potentially deleting legs. 1 1

... ...i ii + 1 i + 1 

... ...n n

Figure 20. The elementary network J(i).

Definition 7.3. Each grounded wiring network P has an associated seed group QP obtained by taking the quotient of the free group generated by the edges of the network by the relation that the counterclockwise product around each vertex of P is 1. The seed torus , AP is the noncommutative torus with QP as its group of coordinate functions. 

Remark 7.4 . The condition on the weights that the counterclockwise product around each vertex is equal to 1 is different from the convention in [GK24]. We will expand on this distinction in Section 17.7. 

Definition 7.5. Let J be a special Jordan algebra with specializing ring R. A J-point of a grounded wiring network P is a group homomorphism QP → R ×.We consider two grounded wiring networks equivalent if they are related by leg slide and identification of legs attached to the same strand. 

Definition 7.6. A leg slide is performed by moving a leg left or right horizontally until it travels across a bridge onto a neighboring strand (Figure 21). A leg slide is associated with the following monomial map on the weights: Let w be the weight of the leg. Each edge that is traversed from ◦ to • has its weight left multiplied by w.Each edge traversed from • to ◦ has its weight right multiplied by w−1.

Definition 7.7. There is a natural mutation of weighted plabic networks given by the square move with a noncommutative generalization given by [GK24, Equation 68]. Definition 7.8. The honeycomb networks , Pn, are a family of networks embedded in a triangle following the pattern demonstrated in Figure 23. The honeycomb networks are used to parameterize triples of GL n(R) flags in [GK24]. We will recover the honeycomb networks when parameterizing groups with an Ap-grading in Section 14.1. These networks are important as they are fully planar, so they can be regrounded. 

Definition 7.9. A full planar triangular grounded network can be regrounded by choosing a new side of the triangle to be the ground side. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 63

(b) n = 4 

Figure 23. The standard GL n-network. In Figure 23, we observe that any side of the triangle can be chosen to be the ground for a honeycomb network. The horizontal strands are identified by “pulling the sides tight” parallel to the ground. 

Definition 7.10. We write |P | for the set of grounded networks which are obtained from 

P by equivalence moves, square moves, leg slides and regroundings. We write A|P | for the noncommutative space obtained by gluing together the seed tori AP along the birational maps defined by each of these operations. From this definition we obtain noncommutative spaces from grounded wiring networks and from polygonal cluster algebras. We refer to both these constructions as noncommutative cluster varieties . Each space is built by gluing seed groups via noncommutative mutation relations. Although the construction of the seed group is different in each case, when we refer to the seed group the correct construction will be clear from context. 

# Part 3. Cluster-like Coordinates on Decorated Flag Varieties 

We will now use Jordan pinnings to define coordinates on configuration spaces of decorated flags. Thus, let G be Jordan split over a root system R over the K Jordan algebras {Ji}. We will write p for the rank of R. We recall that there are 3 cases based on the noncommutative rank of G of type R.

• When G is non-commutative rank 0 of type R, we will see that our coordinates are obtained by evaluating the commutative cluster varieties of [GS19] in field extensions of 

K.

• When G is non-commutative rank 1 of type R, our coordinates will give evaluations of non-commutative polygonal cluster algebras with general r for A1, r = 2 for Bp and 

r = 3 for G2. In this case we will assume that the simple roots in R are indexed such that the noncommutative root is the p-th one. 

• When G is higher noncommutative rank of type R, we will relate our coordinates to the description in [GK24]. Together this amounts to giving for each R a noncommutative cluster variety AR,n whose Jordan points parameterize configurations of n decorated flags. 8. Setup and strategy 

We consider the partial flag variety defined by the associated parabolic PΘ to the R-grading. We denote the flag variety by PΘ := G/P Θ and the decorated flag variety by AΘ := G/U Θ.64 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Moreover, let π : AΘ → PΘ be the projection map induced by the inclusion UΘ ⊂ PΘ. In this case the fiber over a point in PΘ is an LΘ-torsor. The configuration space of n decorated flags for n ∈ N is given by 

Conf n(AΘ) := G\A n

> Θ

using the diagonal action of G on the product. Our ultimate goal is to endow this space with cluster-like coordinates. 8.1. Pairs of flags. We start by considering pairs of flags. Let w0 ∈ W (R) be the element of maximal length which we already encountered in Section 4.4. 

Definition 8.1. Two flags B1, B 2 ∈ PΘ are called transverse if there exists g ∈ G such that 

(gB 1, gB 2) = ( B, w 0B). This is denoted B1 ⋔ B2. Two decorated flags A1, A 2 ∈ AΘ are called transverse if π(A1) ⋔ π(A2).For an element w ∈ W (R), we call a two-tuple of flags, resp. decorated flags, w-transverse if there exits g ∈ G such that (gB 1, gB 2) = ( B, wB ) resp. (gπ (A1), gπ (A2)) = ( B, wB ).

Remark 8.2 . By Proposition 4.38 transversality is a generic condition for pairs of (decorated) flags. But as we saw in Equation (1), for B1, B 2 ∈ PΘ there does not necessarily exist a w in the grading Weyl group such that (gB 1, gB 2) = ( B, wB ). However, all flags that we will consider will be w-transverse for some w in the grading Weyl group. 

Remark 8.3 . If (A1, A 2) is w-transverse, then (A2, A 1) is w−1-transverse. To construct coor-dinates, we will only need the notion of w0-transverse and ςi-transverse where this distinction vanishes. We define the basic invariant for a pair of flags using that the fibers of π are LΘ-torsors: 

Definition 8.4. For two w-transverse decorated flags, we define the L-distance l(A1, A 2) to be the unique element of l ∈ LΘ such that there exists an element g ∈ G for which 

g(A1, A 2) = ( UΘ, wU Θl ) .

This is constant on G-orbits and thus defines a map Conf 2(AΘ) → LΘ. Given a Jordan pinning and choice {Λi} of Jordan weights, we can reconstruct this element of LΘ as 

l = Y

Therefore, our collection of coordinates on Conf ×

(AΘ), the space of transverse configurations, is the set {Λi(l(A1, A 2)} ∈ Q 

> i

Γ( Ji). Before we take a closer look at the L-distance, let us describe the general strategy for the construction of cluster coordinates on Conf n(AΘ).8.2. General strategy. Consider the space Conf × 

> n

(AΘ) ⊂ Conf n(AΘ) defined by the condition that any two flags in the configuration are transverse. For split groups G coordinates on this space were previously constructed: Fock-Goncharov pioneered the approach presented below and described concrete coordinates for G = SL( n, R) [FG06]. This was extended to all classical split algebraic groups by Le [Le19] and further to include exceptional groups by Gilles [Gil21]. Finally, a very general construction was provided by Goncharov-Shen [GS19]. Our goal will be to extend their construction to (G, Θ) reduced of Weyl type. Thus, let [( A1, . . . , A n)] ∈ Conf × 

> n

(AΘ). Its coordinates are obtained as follows: 

Step 1: We associate the flags to the vertices of an n-gon in a counterclockwise manner. 

Step 2: Next, fix a triangulation of the n-gon . This naturally breaks the configuration of n flags into n − 2 configurations of 3 flags (associated to the triangles), which share sub-configurations of 2 flags (associated to the edges). This amalgamation approach was introduced by Fock and Goncharov and they proved that the configurations associated to the triangles determine the full configuration for split G [FG06, Thm. 8.1]. We extend this in Theorem 12.2. Therefore, it suffices to describe coordinates on Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ).

Step 3: We further decompose a configuration of 3 flags into elementary configurations 

(see Section 9). These are elements of Conf 3(AΘ) which are not fully transverse. Instead, NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 65 

one pair of flags is only ςi-transverse for ςi a simple reflection in W (R). See Definition 9.5. These configurations are obtained from a configuration of 3 decorated flags by computing an 

interpolating sequence of decorated flags between one pair of flags in the configuration (or along an edge of the triangle), see Proposition 9.1. It depends on a choice of reduced expression for the longest word w0 ∈ W (R). The set of elementary configurations determines the full configuration. This approach is due to Goncharov-Shen in the split case [GS19], and we extend it in Theorem 11.13. 

Step 4: Invariants of elementary configurations can be obtained by computing pairwise L-distances between the flags. To produce (noncommutative) coordinates from this, one applies the Jordan weights to these L-distances as explained previously. These will be assigned to the nodes of a elementary quiver or edges of an elementary network , which are upgraded version of the ordinary quivers described in [GS19]. Here it will be a polygonal quiver in non-commutative rank 1, see Section 10.2 or a grounded wiring network in higher noncommutative rank Section 7. We will see in Lemma 11.12 that the coordinate functions define a J-point of the corresponding noncommutative cluster variety. 

Step 5: All the elementary seeds are amalgamated to obtain a quiver, with corresponding coordinate functions, first for Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ), and then for Conf × 

> n

(AΘ).

> A2

Figure 24. Overview of the general strategy. Our main goal now is to understand how the choices we made effect these coordinates: (1) the choice of triangulation, (2) the choice of edge within each triangle along which to compute an interpolating sequence, (3) the choice of reduced expression of w0 for each edge, (4) the choice of Jordan weights used to compute coordinates. Specifically, we will show that making different choices correspond to noncommutative muta-tions. We only need to consider a few different elementary operations to make all of the changes above. Concretely, we will prove that we can use mutation to relate coordinate systems 

• on Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) with different reduced expressions (‘2,3,4,6 moves’, Section 11.3) 

• on Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) which differ by the choice of interpolating edge (‘rotation’, Section 11.4) 

• on Conf × 

> 4

(AΘ) arising from the two triangulations of the square (‘flip’, Section 12.1). The choice of Jordan weights only appears in the higher noncommutative rank case and our coordinates will be naturally independent of this choice. 66 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Remark 8.5 . In Section 16, we will see that, when the Jordan algebra Ji has a positive structure, mutations sends positive points to positive points. This will recover the Θ-positive structures on Conf × 

> n

(AΘ) defined in [GW25]. Moreover, the above approach allows to define cluster-like coordinates on the moduli space of decorated twisted local systems and also yields a positive structure on this space as we will see in Section 17. 8.3. 10. Cluster Coordinates on elementary configurations of Θ-flags 

Now that we understand how to reduce a configuration of three decorated flags to a series of elementary configurations, we can start the process of constructing coordinates by first en-dowing the elementary configuration spaces with appropriate coordinates which are suitable for amalgamation in the next step. 10.1. Partial potentials in noncommutative rank 1. We now specialize to noncommu-tative rank 1 , i.e. the Jordan split type is either A1, B p or G2. Observe that in these cases the opposition involution on the (co)root system is trivial, while it may be nontrivial on LΘ.Recall that we index the Dynkin diagram such that the noncommutative root has index p. We denote the corresponding Jordan algebra Jp =: J. All roots βi with i < p are commutative. 

Corollary 10.1. The partial potential a = δ(A, A l, A r ) for a ςp-elementary configuration fulfills 

σ Λp(lr )−1Λp(lb)Λ p(ll)−1 · Λp−1(lr ) = ι(a)

10.2. Elementary seeds in lower noncommutative rank. To each elementary configuration we associate an elementary seed of a polygonal cluster algebra. The cluster coordinates will be given by Jordan weights applied to l(A, A l), l(A, A r ), l(Ar , A l).The quivers of the elementary seed E◦(i) for 1 ≤ i ≤ p will be based on the quivers defined in [GS19]: We think of the plane as having different levels 1, . . . , p . E◦(i) has one node labeled j on every level j̸ = i, and two nodes labeled il, i r on level i. These are connected by an arrow from 

il to ir , and there are half-arrows from ir to i + 1 and i − 1, and from i − 1 and i + 1 to il. The nodes on level p have weight r, on all other levels they have weight 1. The elementary quiver 

E(i) is obtained from E◦(i) by adding an additional frozen node labeled ib of the same weight as the nodes on level i attached in an oriented cycle of full edges. See Figure 27 for examples. 12

L-distance will be the inverse of the weight in the network. 11. Triples of Θ-flags 

The results of the previous section imply the following lemma: 

Lemma 11.1. The {Ji} points of the noncommutative cluster variety AE(i) parameterize the elementary configuration space Conf i

> 3

(AΘ).

Note that there are no mutable vertices or possible square moves at this point, so this cluster variety is really just a noncommutative torus. Now we will amalgamate these cluster varieties into one which describes generic triples of flags. Consider a triple (A1, A 2, A 3) of pairwise transverse flags in AΘ. Then there exist g ∈ G,

l2, l 3 ∈ LΘ and u3 ∈ UΘ such that 

g(A1, A 2, A 3) = ( UΘ, w 0UΘl2, u 3w0UΘl3) .

(AΘ) → LΘ × LΘ × UΘ

(A1, A 2, A 3) 7 → (l2, l 3, u 3)

is an open dense embedding. Proof. The image of the map on the UΘ-factor is UΘ ∩ P opp Θ w0P opp Θ , which is an open dense subset of UΘ. □

We now describe how to assemble the cluster charts for elementary configurations into a cluster chart for a triple of flags: We fix a reduced expression D(w0) = ςi1 · · · ςin for the longest word w0 in W (R). Given a triple (A1, A 2, A 3) ∈ AΘ which represents an element of Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ),we may use this reduced expression to compute an interpolating sequence {Ak 

> 2

} between A2 and 

A3. As we have seen we obtain elementary configurations (A1, A k 

> 2

, A k+1 2 ) in this way, and we constructed elementary seeds/networks for each elementary configuration space. We can now amalgamate these elementary seeds according to the reduced expression D(w0) to obtain a cluster chart for AΘ.11.1. Amalgamating. Depending on the noncommutative rank, we need to amalgamate either polygonal quivers or noncommutative networks. 78 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

11.1.1. Noncommutative rank 1. The seeds for each elementary quiver are planar, and so it makes sense to amalgamate adjacent frozen nodes at the same level. For each k, let k+ be the next index for which ik = ik+ . At the end of the process every node at level ik in the elementary seeds between ik and ik+ will be amalgamated. Due to Corollary 10.3 these nodes are assigned equal functions, and thus every node has an associated function on Conf × 

> 3

(A ). Observe that the extra decoration which turned E(p) into an ordered quiver is chosen such that it makes sense to amalgamate on level p. The half-arrows either combine to give a full arrow if they are oriented the same way or cancel if they have opposite orientations. Internal nodes become unfrozen. The extra nodes on the bottom are connected with half edges oriented from left to right according to the adjacency of the coroots ˇβk in the associated Dynkin diagram. For more details see [GS19]. The resulting quiver has three components of frozen nodes connected by half arrows: the left nodes of the initial elementary seed, the right nodes of the final elementary seed, and the bottom nodes consisting of the extra frozen nodes. By Remark 9.2, ∆p(A3, A 2) = wk−1( ˇβp(A)) where 

wk−1 is the prefix of the reduced expression which returns ˇβp to itself and l(Ak 

> 2

, A k−12 ) = ˇβp(A).As we saw in Lemma 4.34, the action of wk−1 might not be trivial on the special coroot space. This is not a problem, as the difference can only be application of the involution στ . The final step in this construction is then to weave at the frozen weight r node so the function agrees with ∆p(A3, A 2). We compute the action of wk−1 specifically for type Bp in Lemma 13.16 and for type G2 in Lemma 13.24. 11.1.2. Higher noncommutative rank. Our choice of Jordan weights factors into our construction of the interpolating sequence. In higher noncommutative rank we amalgamate our networks simply by gluing their adjacent boundary black vertices on the same strands together. This produces a grounded wiring network with edge weights as the gluing respects the cyclic product condition. However, the amalgamated network has 2p legs and we would like a network with exactly p + 1 legs so that the ground side matches the left and right. Sliding (Definition 7.6) each leg to the left past all the brides identifies a unique strand for each leg. All legs with the same label can be replaced by a single leg whose weight is the product of the weights. By the construction of the system of Jordan weights, the product is independent of the order even though the ring itself is noncommutative. 

Definition 11.3. A grounded wiring network obtained by sliding all the legs to the left of all the bridges and reducing to at most one leg per strand is called left slid .

Proposition 11.4. The left slid grounded wiring network obtained is independent of the choice of system of Jordan weights. Proof. Let fA2 be such that its undecorated flag is the same as A2 but l(A3, fA2) = 1 . Now, every choice of Jordan weights gives the same interpolating sequence between eA2 and A3. The grounded wiring network obtained from amalgamating elementary networks for the interpolating sequence between eA2 and A3 is the same as the left slid network other than the leftmost labels and the legs. Clearly this is true for any choice of Jordan weights. Furthermore, different systems Jordan weights change the decomposition lb = Q ˇβi(Λ i(lb)) but not the final projection to the 

ith component. This projection is the weight of the leg of the ith strand and thus is independent of the choice of Jordan weights. Therefore the left slid network is independent as claimed. □

Corollary 11.5. The amalgamated elementary networks associated to two different choices of Jordan weights are equivalent by leg slides. 

11.2. Cluster charts. The result of the construction is a set of functions on Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) and an ordered quiver or network which indexes the functions. 

Definition 11.6. A set of functions on Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) arising from this construction is called a 

cluster chart for Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ). These functions live on the nodes of an ordered quiver or edges of a network we denote by QD, 1, where D is the reduced expression of w0. We write AD, 1 for the noncommutative torus for which QD, 1 is the group of monomial functions. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 79 

Remark 11.7 . The superscript 1 is used to distinguish these charts from the ‘rotated’ ones that we will introduce in Section 11.4. We note that unlike the commutative charts, these functions satisfy multiplicative relations. Nevertheless they replace the ordinary cluster charts of [GS19] and can be mutated like ordinary cluster charts. 

Definition 11.8. A generalized mutation is any of the following operations from in Part 2. 

• Mutation of polygonal quivers (Definition 6.25) 

• Square moves of weighted networks (Definition 7.7) 

• Switching/ Weaving of noncommutative nodes (Definition 6.14) 

• Leg slides (Definition 7.6) 

• Regrounding a planar network (Definition 7.9) The first two operations are birational transformations and replace the standard mutation of commutative cluster algebras. Switching or weaving at a noncommutative node k results in essentially the same set of functions with a different representative of {Xk, σ (Xk), τ (Xk), στ (Xk)}

chosen for the node. Similarly changing the ground, changes the underlying combinatorial representation but leaves all the functions the same. The final operation of a leg slide is slightly more complicated. It results in “the same chart”, but with the functions changed by a noncommutative monomial transformation. 

Definition 11.9. Let H be a group, h1, h 2 ∈ H. A noncommutative monomial transfor-mation is a map H → H expressed by mh1,h 2 (g) := h1gh −12 .There is another important monomial transformation on cluster charts for AΘ given by the rescaling action (Definition 9.3). 

Proposition 11.10. The rescaling action acts on the functions in any cluster chart via pull back along a noncommutative monomial transformation. Proof. This follows from Proposition 8.8 and Lemma 9.4; the rescaling action rescales all of the flags in the interpolating sequence used to make our cluster charts and the second property of the L−distance is clearly a monomial transformation. □

Our main goal will be to prove: 

Theorem 11.11. The noncommutative cluster variety A|QD, 1| contains a torus for each cluster chart associated to all reduced expressions D′ of w0 and the rotated cluster charts. Its {Ji}

points parameterize open dense subsets of Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ).

11.3. Changing reduced expression. We will now explain how cluster charts coming from different reduced expressions of w0 ∈ W (R) are related. Reduced expressions can be changed from one to another by the following moves [BB05]: 

Proposition 11.14. Let {ςi | 1 ≤ i ≤ p} denote the standard generators of the Weyl group 

W (R) corresponding to the root grading. Any two reduced expressions of w ∈ W can be related by a finite sequence of the following braid moves :

Two Move ςiςj = ςj ςi for |i − j| > 1.

Three Move ςiςi+1 ςi = ςi+1 ςiςi+1 for 1 ≤ i ≤ p − 2

Four Move ςp−1ςpςp−1ςp = ςp−1ςpςp−1ςp (Bp/C p case) 

Six Move ς1ς2ς1ς2ς1ς2 = ς2ς1ς2ς1ς2ς1 (G2 case). 

Thus, to prove Theorem 11.11 it suffices to show that each braid move is realized by a sequence of mutations. 

Lemma 11.15 (Two Move) . The seeds given by two words which differ by a two move only differ in the indexing of the functions. Proof. Let 1 ≤ i, j ≤ p with |i − j| > 1. Clearly, the quiver obtained by amalgamating E(i)

and E(j) is the same as the one obtained by amalgamating E(j) with E(i). According to Corollary 10.3 all the functions can be computed in terms of the two decorated flags with W -distance ςiςj = ςj ςi, and are thus independent of whether we use ςiςj or ςj ςi to obtain the interpolating flag. □

The three move in noncommutative rank 1 is proved essentially the same way as in [GS19]: 

Lemma 11.16 (Three move, NC Rank 1) . Rotation. The notion of positivity in flag varieties is inspired by tuples on the circle which follow an orientation. In this case any cyclic rearrangement preserves this notion of positivity. This is also true for generic configurations in PΘ but not in decorated flag varieties. Instead, we need to use the central element sG ∈ G:

Definition 11.17. The twisted cyclic shift map tw : Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) → Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) is defined by 

tw : ( A1, A 2, A 3) 7 → (sGA2, A 3, A 1) .

We can obtain additional cluster charts of Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) by precomposing the above construc-tion with the twisted cyclic shift: 

Definition 11.18. The rotated cluster charts are obtained by applying the previous con-struction to the triples tw( A1, A 2, A 3), respectively tw 2(A1, A 2, A 3). The coordinate functions are indexed by quivers/networks QD, 2, respectively QD, 3, where D is a reduced expression of 

w0 ∈ W (R).Clearly, all the results about the original cluster charts also apply to the rotated ones. For the discussion of decorated twisted local systems in Section 17 the following result is crucial: 

Proposition 11.19. The twisted cyclic shift is realized by a sequence of generalized mutations. Proof. This is trivial to prove in the A1 case as our cluster charts are already rotationally symmetric up to the orientation of the edges of the triangle. But the choices were made exactly so that the difference between the ordered quivers is a switch at the corresponding node, while the functions are related by the opposition involution, which is exactly σ in the A1 case. The Ap case is proved by changing the choice of ground in the honeycomb GL p+1 network (Figure 23). The GL p+1 network is the network constructed using the word ς1 · ς2ς1 · ς3ς2ς1 ·82 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

. . . · ςpςp−1 . . . ς 1. So we first use braid transformations to move to this chart, then we change the choice of ground, and finally we undo the braid transformations. The Cp case follows from the A2p−1 case since in this case our charts are really just the same as charts for groups of type 

A2p−1 and we can rotate using the same procedure. For the Bp and G2 cases we prove this in Sections 13.2.7 and 13.3.1 via an explicit calculation. This is slightly more involved even for the edge functions since the opposition involution is not always σ but can also be τ on Γ( J), in which case one has to weave and switch at the corresponding node. □

Corollary 11.20. If c ∈ Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) is a positive configuration, so is tw( c).

11.5. Changing orientations. Given a triple (A1, A 2, A 3) ∈ A 3Θ, we have assigned a total order given by A1 → A3 → A2 in the construction of cluster charts, and we have computed the functions associated to frozen edges accordingly: If Ai → Aj , the functions on the corresponding edge are given by Λi(Ai, A j ). We have seen that for the rotated cluster charts the edge functions are related to the original ones via the opposition involution. In this spirit we could upgrade the construction of cluster charts even further by allowing arbitrary orientations for the edges of the triangle and computing the edge functions as follows: If the orientation of an edge Ai → Aj is compatible with the total order, we assign the functions 

Λi(Ai, A j ), otherwise we assign Λi(Aj , A i)∗.

Example 11.21 . Consider the triangle with the side edges oriented “up” from A2 → A1 and 

A3 → A1 when G is B2-graded. In Figure 33, we see the polygonal quiver for the standard case and with the edge orientation changed. In this case the opposition involution fixes the root system, but acts via the involution τ on the p = 2 root space (Corollary 13.13). We observe that in order to amalgamate the elementary quiver to the sides, we first weave and switch at the boundary. Note these operations preserve the angle and the partial potential.

Example 11.22 . We now consider a similar example when G is A2-graded. In this group, the opposition involution acts by reversing the order of components and applying the inverse: l =[A, B, C ] 7 → [C−1, B −1, A −1]). We are now prepared to conclude the detailed explanation of the construction of cluster coordinates on Conf × 

> n

(AΘ) which we outlined in Section 8.2. Thus let (A1, . . . , A n) represent an element of Conf × 

> n

(AΘ). Recall that we start by associating the flags to the vertices of an n-gon in a counterclockwise manner. Next, fix a triangulation of the n-gon. We will use this data to obtain an element of Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) for every triangle as follows: First, orient all arcs of the triangulation, including the sides of the n-gon via the total order given by reading clockwise around the n-gon: 

A1 → An → An−1 → · · · → A3 → A2 .NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 83 

Given a triangle T , let i, j, k be the vertices of T ordered such that Ai → Ak → Aj in the total order we just defined. Then the configuration associated to T is represented by the triple 

(Ai, A j , A k) ∈ A 3Θ.For every triangle we choose one side and a reduced expression of the longest element w0 ∈

W (R). Using the construction explained in Section 11 we obtain a (possibly rotated) cluster chart on Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) which we evaluate on the configuration associated to the triangle. For every triangle we have a seed which we view as inscribed in it. We then amalgamate these seeds along the internal arc of the triangulation. As explained in Section 11.5, in the Bp and 

G2 cases we may need to weave/switch so that the edge functions for the individual triangles become compatible with the orientations of the sides. In the Ap and Cp cases, we need to amalgamate our seeds by gluing the networks along the boundary • vertices with the same index on the edges of the triangles. Recall that by construction, the number of legs is equal to the number of strands and each leg has a unique index given by performing a full slide to the left. In this way we obtain an ordered quiver or network inscribed into the n-gon whose nodes index a set of functions on Conf × 

> n

(AΘ).

Definition 12.1. A set of functions on Conf × 

> n

(AΘ) arising from the above construction is called a cluster chart . We will write AR,n for the noncommutative cluster variety associated to (any) cluster chart. Recall that R here refers to the Jordan split type of the group we are considering.

(AΘ).Proof. Very nice charts are given by the fan triangulation , the oriented triangulation for which all internal arcs start at A1, see Figure 34. Any point in Conf ×

(AΘ) → Ln−1Θ × U n−2Θ . Now we simply extend the proof of Theorem 11.13: If u3, . . . , u n lie in the image of the Lusztig map, they can be reconstructed from the coordinates one by one, just like the elements l2, . . . , l n. Then Proposition 16.10 shows that a positive J-point reconstructs a positive ui and li. This is the definition of a positive tuple of decorated flags. In order to extend this proof to charts for arbitrary triangulations, we will see that any two such charts are connected by series of cluster mutations (Theorem 12.3). The resulting map from the set parametrized by one cluster chart to the set parametrized by the other is defined on an open dense subset. Moreover such mutations map positive J-points to positive J-points. □84 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

12.1. Flip. In order to extend the proof of Theorem 12.2 for the fan triangulation to any cluster chart, we will prove: 

Theorem 12.3. Any two cluster charts for Conf × 

> n

(AΘ) are related by general mutations. 

We already proved Theorem 11.11, which accounts for the different choices within each in-dividual triangles. Since flips relate any two triangulations, it suffices to prove that flips in the triangulation can be realized by generalized mutations. To understand the flip, we adapt Goncharov-Shen’s approach by considering four flags on a triangulated square, where we take interpolating sequences across the top and bottom edges ([GS19, Section 11]). In this case, the elementary configurations on the top can be identified with flipped opposite elementary configurations (Definition 9.10). These amalgamate nicely with any of the standard elemen-tary configurations allowing for many mixed charts. These charts are parametrized by words in W (R) × W (R), where we denote the generators of the bottom edge by ςi := ( ςi, 1) and the generators of the top edge by ςi := (1 , ς i∗ ) for 1 ≤ i ≤ p.By amalgamating the corresponding elementary and opposite elementary charts, we associate a cluster chart to every reduced expression of (w0, w 0) ∈ W (R) × W (R), similar to Figure 36. In the initial setup, all elements of the form ςi come before elements of the form ςi and after the flip the order is reversed. Thus, to prove Theorem 12.3 it suffices to prove that the following moves on these cluster charts can be realized by mutations: 

Two Move ςiςj = ςj ςi for i̸ = j.

Twisted Two Move ςiςi = ςiςi.The two move in this extended version, can be treated the same as before (Lemma 11.15). 

Lemma 12.4. The seeds given by two words which differ by a twisted two move ii → ii are related by a single mutation on the cluster chart E(ii ).

13. Noncommutative rank 1 detailed examples 

We will now explain the detailed calculations missing in the preceding proofs case by case. 13.1. Type A1. The abstract construction becomes much simpler in Jordan split type A1. In fact, we recover the coordinates on the space of positive configurations of decorated flags which were constructed for G ≃ Sp(2 n, R) in [AGRW21]. The polygonal cluster algebras reduce to the noncommutative surface cluster algebra of Berenstein and Retakh [BR18]. 13.1.1. Jordan-pinning. Let G be a Jordan-split group over A1. In Section 4.1 we defined a Jordan pinning of G to consist of the following three maps: 

x : V → G, y : V → G, ˇβ : Γ( J) → G . 

We assume now that the Jordan pinning provides an isomorphism of groups G ∼= SL 2(J), so that the weight Λβ : G0 → R is well-defined as the dual to ˇβ.

Example 13.1 . Consider the Jordan algebra J = H(R, σ ) for R a ring with involution σ. Then 

V = Rσ and eΓ( J) = R×. As both Rσ and R× naturally sit inside R, we can represent G as a subgroup Sp 2(R, σ ) inside Mat 2(R) as defined in [AGRW21]. The pinning maps become for

l(A2, A 1) = − σ(l(A1, A 2))

Λ([ A, C ], [B, D ]) = σ(A)D − σ(C)B.

In Jordan split type A1 there is a unique reduced expression of the longest word in the Weyl group, w0 = ςβ . Moreover, the interpolating sequence of flags is trivial as 

(A1, A 2, A 3) is already an elementary configuration of type ςβ . Therefore the cluster chart for a triple of decorated Θ-flags is given by 

A1

A2 A3

Let Mij = Λ β (l(Ai, A j )) be the cluster coordinate associated to the directed side (i, j ).

Theorem 13.8. The potential of the configuration (A1, A 2, A 3) is given by the angle 

(15) ∆g (M23 ) = σ(M12 )−1M23 M −113 .

Proof. The configuration (A1, A 2, A 3) is equivalent to (UΘ, ω 0UΘM12 , x (v)ω0UΘM13 ) where v

is the potential. We then compute M23 = Λ β (l(A2, A 3)) = σ(M12 )vM 13 and the formula follows. □

Remark 13.9 . In this case, the angle in the polygonal cluster algebra is the noncommutative angle of a noncommutative surface cluster algebra. The fact that the angle belongs to the vector space of the Jordan algebra is equivalent to the angle being fixed by σ for J = H(R, σ ).The final step to understanding a triple of flags is understanding twisted cyclic rotation. This case is very simple, as we observe that the only change to this seed under rotation is the orientation of the arcs bounding the triangular tile. As seeds of a polygonal cluster algebra this corresponds to performing a switch at these arcs. This changes the cluster coordinate by applying σ, which has, by Lemma 13.2, exactly the same effect as computing Λβ (l(sGAj , A i)) 

instead of Λβ (l(Ai, A j )) as needed. 13.1.4. Tuples of flags. In Section 12, we saw that flipping an arc of the triangulation amounted to a series of moves on reduced expressions of the longest word in W (R) × W (R). The new move for Θ-flags was the twisted two move at the noncommutative node. In Jordan split type 

A1, this move suffices to flip the arc. The proof of the twisted two move in Lemma 12.4 relied on the additivity of the potential, which we saw in Section 13.1.3 is equivalent to the noncommutative surface angles. Consider a four tuple (A1, A 2, A 3, A 4) of pairwise transverse flags. The additivity of the potentials implies that (16) σ(M −114 )M42 M −112 = σ(M −113 )M32 M −112 + σ(M −114 )M43 M −113 .

This in turn implies the following non commutative exchange relation: (17) M42 = σ(M14 )σ(M −113 )M32 + M43 M −113 M12 .NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 91 

Remark 13.10 . This is exactly the exchange relation on a noncommutative surface given in [BR18]. Our next step is to study the case of Jordan split type Bp, where more of the subtleties of the general construction appear. 13.2. Type Bp. We now consider G a group of Jordan split type Bp.A root system of type Bp contains 2p short roots and 2p(p − 1) long roots. We chose a set of simple roots for the Θ-root system, {β1, · · · , β p−1, β p} with standard indexing coming from the Dynkin diagram so that βp is the unique short root. The long roots correspond to commutative Jordan algebras, and are therefore associated with a finite extension of K we will denote by K′. The short root spaces, {± βp, ±(βp +

βp−1), . . . , ± P 

> i

βi} can be associated to a degree 2 Jordan algebra of Clifford type, J(V, b, e )

for V a K′ vector space, b a bilinear form, and e an element of norm 1.The structure group is the even Clifford group Γ0(V, b ). We recall that the map embedding the vector space into the structure group is given by ι(v) = ve .The vector space V comes with its adjugate involution which we denote by τ . Since our Jordan algebra is of degree 2, this map is a linear map. Similarly, the generalized structure group comes with the two anti-involutions σ, τ . These are given explicitly by the standard transposition map on the Clifford algebra 

τ (x) = xT , and σ(x) = ex T e . 

Since ι(τ (v)) = τ (ι(v)) we are justified in using the same name for both anti-involutions. More-over we find that LΘ is a central extension of Γ0(V, b ) and we extend σ and τ to anti-involutions on LΘ by having them act trivially on the center. By further abuse of notation, we denote the resulting maps by σ and τ again. 13.2.1. Θ-pinning. Choose a Jordan pinning for G as discussed in Section 4.2. Since the Jordan algebra is always of Clifford type, the pinning maps have the following form: For 1 ≤ i < p xi : K′ → G yi : K′ → G ˇβi : K′× → G , 

and 

xp : V → G yp : V → G ˇβp : Γ 0(V, b ) → G . 

We recall that the pinning provides lifts of the grading Weyl group to G via: 

ςi = yi(1) xi(−1) yi(1) ςp = yp(1)xp(−1)yp(1) .

We will use these to compute some identities for the Jordan pinning in this concrete case. 13.2.2. Weyl group. There are two nice reduced expressions of the longest word we will use in our calculations. The first is given by p repetitions of the Coxeter element ς1ς2 · · · ςp. The second word is inductive and consists of multiplying the p distinct mountain blocks ,

Mk = ςkςk+1 · · · ςp−1ςpςp−1 · · · ςk+1 ςk .

The mountain blocks provide a nontrivial action on the special root space up.

(18) Mk · xp(v) = xp(τ (v)) .

uβp . The action of ςp does not change the image root space and thus the action w−1 returns us to uβp . As the pinning map is surjective, there is some w ∈ V such that xp(w) = Mk · xp(v).We then compute the action on xp(v)ςp using Lemma 4.44:

The Gauss decomposition of elements is unique, so we equate the argument of the coroot. This implies ι(w) = στ (ι(v)) = ι(τ (v)) . Since ι is injective we then have w = τ (v) as claimed. □

13.2.7. Rotation. This section is dedicated to the proof of Proposition 11.19 for the Bp case. The case p = 2 has incidentally already been proven in the previous section. We will do this by considering a particular reduced expressions of w0. Let M = M1M2 . . . M p

be the reduced expression given by the product of the mountain blocks in order and let C =(ς1 · · · ςp)p be the Coxeter word. We denote by M ′, C′ the reversed words. 

Proposition 13.22. The cluster charts QiM and Qi+1   

> M′

are related by a sequence of cluster mutations at commutative nodes and switches and weaves at noncommutative nodes 

Proving this proposition will allow us to change to any rotated cluster chart since we can use two moves, three moves and four moves to change Q2  

> M′

into Q2 

> M

and then apply the same sequence to change to Q3  

> M′

.

Proof. In the split real case ( G = Spin( p, p + 1) ) Le [Le19] constructs an explicit sequence of mutations which relate the cluster charts Q1 

> C

and Q2 

> C′

. We will denote this sequence by L.There are two important facts to know about this sequence: (1) It only mutates at small nodes. (2) It flips all the arrows between big nodes which correspond to digons in Figure 39, i.e. reverses their direction and changes their color. The following lemma is a simple exercise in rearranging reduced expressions: 

Lemma 13.23. The reduced expression M can be changed into C only by using two moves and three moves. 

Let R be the sequence of mutations realizing the change from Q1 

> M

to Q1 

> C

and R′ the sequence which moves Q2 

> C′

to Q2  

> M′

. Our candidate sequence is given by RLR ′ (read as a sequence of mutations from left to right). Since the mutation corresponding to the three move does not change any arrows between the noncommutative nodes, this composite sequence also has the effect of flipping all of the arrows which are in the digons. We now want to compare directly the functions of our mutated chart RLR ′(Q1 

> M

) and those of 

Q2  

> M′

. Due to Le’s work we know that the functions on the small nodes transform correctly, and moreover we know that the norms of the noncommutative coordinates have not changed (and hence have transformed correctly). Therefore, we can make our lives much easier by considering a seed for which all of the commutative nodes and every norm is equal to 1 and explicitly computing the transformation. Consider the triple of flags (A1, A 2, A 3) = ( UΘ, w 0UΘl2, uw 0UΘl3) such that l2 = ˇβp(A), l 3 =ˇβp(B) and u = FM (t) such that N (A) = N (B) = 1 and all the real entries of t are 1 and the vector entries given in order by v1, . . . , v p with N (vi) = 1 . We will denote for convenience 

Vi := ι(vi) and we recall that σ(Vi) = Vi. We will also denote for convenience l∗ := στ p−1(l) for elements of LΘ.Then we can compute all of the coordinates of Q1

13.3. Type G2. We now consider Jordan split type G2. As in Jordan split type Bp we only need to prove two lemmas in order to use the general theory to construct cluster charts for arbitrary configurations of flags. 

Theorem 11.11: The two charts on triples of flags related by the six move are connected by a sequence of cluster mutations. 

Proposition 11.19: Two charts related by twisted cyclic shift are connected by a sequence of cluster mutations. In this case, G is associated to a commutative Jordan algebra given by a field extension 

K′ associated to the root β1 and a degree 3 Jordan algebra over K′ which we denote simply by J = ( V, ι, 1) which is associated to the short root β2. The grading Weyl group, W (R), is generated by ς1, ς 2. For ease of reading in this section, we will denote β1 = α and β2 = β.For any Jordan algebra of degree 3, the norm map is degree 3, and the adjugate map is degree 2. We can define a trilinear form 3N (u, v, w ) := N (u + v + w) − N (u + v) − N (u + w) − N (v +

w) + N (u) + N (v) + N (w), and a bilinear product 2( v × w) := τ (v + w) − τ (v) − τ (w). This bilinear product is called the Freudenthal product. A choice of Jordan pinning consists of the following compatible collection of maps: 

x1 : K′ → Gx2 : V → G

ˇα : K′× → G

ˇβ : Γ( J) → Gy1 : K′ → Gy2 : V → G . 

There are two different Lusztig maps coming from the two reduced expressions of w0, D1, D 2:

F1(a, u, b, v, c, w ) = x1(a)x2(u)x1(b)x2(v)x1(c)x2(w)

F2(u, a, v, b, w, c ) = x2(u)x1(a)x2(v)x1(b)x2(w)x1(c)

with a, b, c ∈ K′ and u, v, w ∈ V .

Lemma 13.24. The opposition involution v → v∗ on uβ is trivial. Proof. It suffices to compute the action of ς2w0 = ς1ς2ς1ς2ς1 on ˇβ(v) using Lemmas 4.34 and 4.35. 

ς1ς2ς1ς2ς1 · ˇβ(v) = ς1ς2ς1ς2 · ˇα(N (v)) ˇβ(v) = ς1ς2ς1 · ˇα(N (v)) ˇβ(τ (v)) = ς1ς2 · ˇα(N (v)) ˇβ(τ (v)) = ς1 · ˇα(N (v)) ˇβ(v) = ˇβ(v) .

As in Corollary 13.13, the action on the coroot induces the action on the root space. □102 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD

Proposition 13.28 (Proposition 11.19 for G2). The twisted cyclic shift and the 6-move are realized by polygonal cluster mutations in Jordan split type G2.Proof. Each of the mutation sequences S, T are involutions. Starting at Q1 

> D1

> D1

> D2
