Remark 13.29 . The final mutated cluster variables appear to be rational functions in the fac-torization parameters. One can use the trilinear form and Freudenthal product to rewrite them as as polynomials in the factorization parameters, compare to [GW26b] 14. Higher noncommutative rank 

The key difference between groups of higher noncommutative rank and the previous groups is the lack of a unique system of Jordan weights. Previously the system of Jordan weights were used to define the coordinates associated to the nodes of the cluster chart. Instead, we use a Jordan pinning of the group to construct a noncommutative grounded wiring network (Section 7) which will parameterize configurations of flags, elements of G, and surface representations. This description will match the noncommutative cluster structure of [GK24]. 14.1. Type Ap. Recall that the recognition theorem (Theorem 3.13) states that for p > 2 any 

Ap graded Lie algebra is a central extension of sl p+1 (D) for an associative unital algebra D

over a field K. The corresponding Θ-simply connected group is SL p+1 (D). However it will be convenient to consider the central extension GL p+1 (D) of invertible (p + 1) × (p + 1) matrices with entries in D as well. We will assume for now that if p = 2 then we are in the classical case, so that G is not a form of the group E6.14.1.1. Θ-pinning. We now construct a Jordan pinning as discussed in Section 4.2. In this case, each Jordan algebra Ji is of associative type and we identify the structure group Γ( J) with a subgroup of D× × D×. Then a choice of pinning maps is the following:

14.2.2. Four move. We now wish to prove Theorem 11.11 for Cp. For i < p , the three move 

ςi−1ςiςi−1 7 → ςiςi−1ςi is realized by two and three moves in the unfolded A2p−1 network. There-fore the calculation in Section 14.1.2 covers these cases. The remaining case is the four move 

ςp−1ςpςp−1ςp 7 → ςpςp−1ςpςp−1. This move lives inside a left slid network of type C2 and so it suffices to verify the following: 

Lemma 14.4. The left slid grounded wiring networks of type C2 for D1 = ςp−1ςpςp−1ςp and 

D2 = ςpςp−1ςpςp−1 are related by a sequence of four square moves. Proof. We begin by lifting both words to words in the A3 Weyl group. These words are related by a sequence of two moves and three moves and thus can be realized by a sequence of square moves. In Figure 50, we show an explicit sequence of square moves in faces 3, 2, 1, 3 which realizes the transformation from D1 to D2. Since the weighted network associated to a given word and choice of Jordan weights is unique it must be the case that the weights of the final network agree with those on the chart associated to D2 even though we passed though asymmetric charts to get there. □

Figure 50. Realizing the four move with square moves. 14.2.3. Rotation and Cp networks. We already proved that rotation by mutation for groups of type Cp is possible by simply rotating the associated chart in for the group of type A2p−1 which contains it. However, we make here an interesting observation. Groups of type B2 can also be considered as type C2 where the Jordan algebra on the long root is commutative. For type B2 we rotate in an entirely different way; a single mutation at either the small or large node has the effect of flipping to a rotated chart with the other reduced word, recall Figure 42. However in the C2 case we are entirely unable to do either of these options. In Figure 50 we see that we cannot mutate at the square labeled 2 (which would be the small node for B2) and we cannot mutate at square 1 immediately after mutating at 3 and obtain a network corresponding to a reduced expression in A3 (which would be like mutating at the large node). We propose the following remedy: define mutation at the square 2 by the composition of square moves and regrounding which rotates the C2 chart QD1,1 to QD2,2 and mutation at squares 1 and 3 by the composition moving QD1,1 to QD2,3. The composition of these two mutations clearly has order 3 in analogy to the B2 case. We could try to define a whole theory of type Cp networks using these rules as mutations. We note that one must work with grounded networks to make sense of this since the new composite mutations involve regrounding the network. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 111 

14.3. Exceptional Groups. The exceptional groups with A2, C3 and F4 grading cannot be described using noncommutative networks or polygonal cluster algebras. They light their own path, following their own exceptional rules. However, our rules for constructing cluster charts still gives us a collection of L-distances which parametrize a given configuration of flags. However we are not aware of a simple set of rules for changing between charts that we could call cluster transformations. It would be interesting to try and isolate such a set of transformations. They would include 

• Octonionic 3 move for A2,

• Octonionic rotation for A2,

• Octonionic rotation for C3,

• Noncommutative rotation for F4.The flip and 4-move actually are already understood as they live in A1 and C2 types respectively. 

Remark 14.5 . The quaternionic F4 case is interesting to consider since it both contains a B3

which is given by a noncommutative polygonal cluster algebra and a C3 which is given by a noncommutative network. Somehow the correct structure here would marry these two worlds together. 

# Part 4. Applications 

We will now discuss some concrete applications of the theory developed in the preceding parts. First, we will use the coordinates from Part 3 to obtain coordinates on subsets of UΘ and G. We use the combinatorics to compute the Gauss decomposition of group elements, their opposite decomposition, as well as the decomposition of the product. This has useful applications to positivity which we will discuss in the subsequent section. There we will consider the special cases in which (G, Θ) is such that all Jordan algebras admit a positive structure. Then a positive Jordan pinning induces a positive structure on G and its decorated flag variety, which is actually the same as described in [GW25]. We will discuss how the cluster coordinates from Part 3 detect this positivity on the decorated flag variety. Using the previous discussion we will apply this to detect positivity of elements in G, allowing us to obtain alternative proofs for some statements about the positive semigroups from [GW25, GW26a, GW26b]. On the other hand, we will explain how to extend the coordinates to moduli spaces of decorated local systems. 15. Cluster-like coordinates on groups 

We will provide coordinates on an open dense subset of G using our previous construction of coordinates on Conf × 

> 4

(AΘ). This will eventually also give us positivity tests when G has a 

Θ-positive structure in the sense of Guichard-Wienhard [GW25]: given an element g ∈ G, we can decide whether g is in the positive semigroup by evaluating certain functions on G in g.15.1. 15.5. Generalized double R-Bruhat cells. We can generalize the cluster structure given on the full group to any double R-Bruhat cell, Cu,v = PΘuP Θ ∩ P opp Θ vP opp Θ for u, v ∈ W (R).

Definition 15.8. Let Conf u,v  

> 4

(AΘ) be the configurations of decorated flags (A1, A 2, A 3, A 4) ∈

Conf 4(AΘ) which satisfy (1) (A1, A 2) and (A4, A 3) are w0-transverse, (2) (A1, A 4) are u-transverse and (A2, A 3) are v-transverse. We write generators of W (R)×W (R) as i = ( e, ς i) and i = ( ςi∗ , e ). Given a reduced expression for (u, v ) we can make a cluster chart by replacing each generator i (resp. i) with E(i) (resp. 

E(¯ i)) and amalgamating. Denote by Au,v R the noncommutative cluster variety which has this as an initial seed. 

Theorem 15.9. 

(1) Au,v R is independent of the choice of reduced expression of (u, v ).(2) The {Ji} points of each cluster chart of Au,v R parameterize a dense open subset of 

Conf u,v  

> 4

(AΘ).(3) There is an injective map IΘ : Cu,v ∗

→ Conf u,v  

> 4

(AΘ) given by 

IΘ(g) = ( UΘ, w 0UΘ, gw 0UΘ, gU Θ).

The configurations in the image satisfy l(A1, A 2) = l(A4, A 3) = 1.(4) The {Ji} points of each cluster chart which land in the image of IΘ parameterize an open subset of Cu,v ∗

.Proof. The cluster variety is independent of the choice of reduced expression for (u, v ) by The-orem 11.11 and Theorem 12.3 which showed any two reduced expressions are related by cluster mutation. The second statement follows from our construction of the cluster charts; each cluster chart is built using elementary pieces which parameterize elementary configurations. Gluing these configurations gives the appropriate configuration space. For the third statement, first observe that the pair (PΘ, gP Θ) is u transverse if and only if 

g ∈ PΘuP Θ. Similarly, the pair (P opp Θ , gP opp Θ ) is v-transverse if and only if g ∈ P opp Θ v∗P opp Θ .So both transversality conditions occur exactly when g ∈ Cu,v ∗

and IΘ(g) lies in Conf u,v  

> 4

(AΘ).Moreover, the elements in the image of IΘ satisfy the condition on L-distances. To see that the map is injective, we observe that a configuration of flags in Conf u,v  

> 4

(AΘ) can be represented as a quadruple in (G/U Θ)4 in a unique way so that A1 = UΘ and A2 = w0UΘ with A3 and A4

fixed. Since the pairs (A1, A 2) and (A3, A 4) have the same transversality and L distance there must be a unique element of G which sends the pair (UΘ, w 0UΘ) to (A4, A 3). For each point in the image of IΘ, this determines a unique an element g ∈ Cu,v ∗

.The fourth statement now follows from the previous three statements. The {Ji} points describing configurations which land in the image of IΘ can be pulled back by IΘ to parameterize an open dense subset of Cu,v ∗

. □

The noncommutative cluster variety Au,v R is a noncommutative deformation of the cluster structures on double Bruhat cells for split groups described in [BFZ05]. The R = Ap case should recover much of the theory of noncommutative double Bruhat decompositions described in [BR05]. Furthermore, we expect that one can continue this entire story to a theory of “ R-Braid varieties” as a generalization of [CGG +25]. 16. Positivity 

For a split algebraic group G over a field K which contains a positive subset K>0, Lusztig introduced a positive semigroup G>0, as well as positive semigroups in the unipotent subgroup 

U∆ and U opp ∆ [Lus94]. In particular, this provides positive semigroups for any split-real Lie group using R>0. The construction relies on a pinning of the group G. We will now explain how a Jordan pinning can provide more general positive semigroups. This includes Lusztig’s construction and recover Guichard-Wienhard’s positive structures with respect to Θ [GW25]. 116 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

16.1. Positive Θ-pinnings. Recall from Definition 2.21 that a positive structure on a qua-dratic Jordan algebra J over a formally real field K is given by a choice of positive cone 

V >0 ⊂ V ×, which is closed under addition, scalar multiplication by positive elements, and inverse, and moreover contains 1 and is preserved by the action of the structure group Γ( J).If each isotopy class of Jordan algebra appearing contains a Jordan algebra with positive structure, we have a distinguished class of Jordan pinnings. 

Definition 16.1. A positive Jordan pinning of G with respect to a root system R is a Jordan pinning such that each Ji admits a positive structure. A choice of positive structure on each Ji identifies a positive cone in each simple R-root space by x(V >0 

> i

). This is reminiscent of Guichard-Wienhard’s notion of positivity with respect to Θ:Let Θ be a subset of simple restricted roots of a real Lie group G and ZΘ be the center of the corresponding Levi-subgroup. Let uβ be a weight space of the adjoint action of ZΘ on g.

Definition 16.2. G has a Θ-positive structure if each uβ contains a proper convex cone 

> ◦

c (uβ ) ⊂ uβ which is invariant under the action of L◦

> Θ

, the connected component of the identity in the Levi subgroup. It is not immediately obvious that the pairs (G, Θ) for which the grading induced by PΘ on G

admits a positive Jordan pinning are the same as those for which G admits a Θ-positive structure. However, we can obtain the same classification: Simply combine the classification of Jordan-compatible Θ (Figure 5) with that of Jordan algebras with a positive structure (Proposition 2.23) to obtain: 

Theorem 16.3 ([GW25],Theorem 1.1) . A simple Lie group, G, with root system grading induced by PΘ admits a positive Jordan pinning (respectively a Θ-positive structure) if and only if (G, Θ) 

belongs to the following list: (1) (Split Real) G is split real and Θ = ∆ .(2) (Type A1) G is a Hermitian Lie group of tube type (with restricted root system Cr ) and 

Θ = {αr } the unique long simple root. (3) (Type Bp) G is locally isomorphic to SO( p + 1 , q + p + 1) (with restricted root system 

Bp+1 ) and Θ = {α1, . . . , α p} the collection of all long simple roots. (4) (Type G2) G is one of the real forms F (4) 4 , E (2) 6 , E (−5) 7 , E (−24) 8 whose restricted root system is type F4 and Θ = {α1, α 2} is the set of long simple roots. Remark 16.4 . Observe that all pairs (G, Θ) in the classification are of NC rank 0 (split real) or NC rank 1 but never of higher noncommutative rank. 16.2. Positive semigroups. This proof hints already at a notion of positive configurations of decorated flags, which can be detected by our coordinates. We will explore this next. 16.3. Configurations of Flags. Throughout this section assume that (G, Θ) is such that the grading on G induced by PΘ admits a positive Jordan pinning. Then the positive structure on

Remark 16.9 . This setup also covers the case where G is split real and Θ = ∆ . Then, all the definitions agree with those given in [FG06]. More concretely, take PΘ = B, a Borel subgroup with unipotent subgroup UΘ = U , and use the Weyl group W = W (R) to define transverse flags in this case. The fibers of the projection π : A → B are H-torsors, where H = B ∩ Bopp is a Cartan subgroup. We can obtain a characterization of Θ-positive configurations of 3 decorated flags in terms of the cluster charts constructed in Part 3: 

Proposition 16.10. A configuration in Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) is positive if and only if it is parameterized by a positive J-point. Explicitly, all the functions from any cluster chart take values in R>0,respectively the identity component of Γ0(J) and all the angles take values in V >0.Proof. Suppose a configuration in Conf × 

> 3

(AΘ) is parameterized by a positive J-point. Such a configuration can be represented as (UΘ, w 0UΘl2, uw 0UΘl3) with u ∈ UΘ, l2, l 3 ∈ LΘ. The elements l2 and l3 are computed as the product of ˇβi(si) where si is in the identity component by assumption. Thus l2, l 3 ∈ L◦

> Θ

. Furthermore u = FD (t1, · · · , t n) where ti are the partial potentials which are positive by assumption. Thus u ∈ U >0Θ as needed. Conversely, suppose (A1, A 2, A 3) = ( UΘ, w 0UΘl2, uw 0UΘl3) represents a positive configura-tion, i.e. l2, l 3 ∈ L◦ 

> Θ

and u = F (t1, . . . , t n) with ti ∈ K>0 or V >0 appropriately. Then the functions Λi(A1, A 2), Λi(A1, A 3) and Λi(A3, A 2) take values in K>0 or Γ◦(J). To see that all the other functions fulfill the positivity conditions, we proceed inductively along the interpola-tion sequence. At each elementary configuration we have the that all coordinates are positive except for the rightmost node. Moreover the potential of this configuration is tik is assumed to be positive. Thus the equation in Lemma 10.4 can be rearranged to recover the rightmost function as a product of positive functions. It remains to verify every other angle in each polyg-onal tile is positive. By construction, one angle in each tile recovers the potential and thus is positive. All other angles are obtained by operations which preserve the positive cone, since all the coordinates have been shown to lie in R>0, respectively the identity component of Γ0(J). □

Remark 16.11 . The positivity criterion from Proposition 16.10 is not minimal in the sense that there are too many conditions to check. From Proposition 11.2 we expect that it should be sufficient to check 2p + n conditions where n is the length of the longest word. Let k be the number of times p occurs in the reduced expression of the longest word. This is an invariant of the (non-split) Jordan split type (independent of the reduced expression) and is equal to 1 in type A1, p in type Bp and 3 in type G2. This number is also the number of polygonal tiles in any cluster chart for the triple of flags. 

Corollary 16.12. A configuration in Conf × 

> 3

(A ) is positive if and only if it fulfills the following 

2p + n conditions: (1) All the n − p + 1 − k functions associated to small nodes in the interior of the triangle take values in K>0.(2) The p − 1 function associated to small nodes on the bottom edge of the triangle take values in K>0.(3) The p functions on the left and right edges of the triangle take values in K>0 or Γ( J)◦

depending on the weight. (4) One angle in each of the k polygonal tiles is equal to ι(v) for v ∈ V >0.NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 119 

Proof. The forward implication is clear as the 2p + n conditions are a subset of the conditions in Proposition 16.10. To show the reverse implication, suppose that a configuration represented by (A1, A 2, A 3) fulfills the smaller set of conditions. We prove that this implies the full set of conditions from Proposition 16.10. First, we prove that all functions in the interior associated to weight r nodes take values in Γ( J)◦. This is proved inductively from each edge for each elementary seed of the form E◦(p). One angle in this tile is assumed to be positive. Moreover the angle can be expressed as a monomial where only one coordinate is not assumed to be in 

Γ( J)◦ by inductive assumption. As Γ( J)◦ is a subgroup, this proves the missing coordinate lies in Γ( J)◦ as well. The elementary seed E(p) occurs only once, and so the inductive proof above is enough to guarantee the left and right nodes are in Γ( J)◦. This leaves one missing node, the weight r node on the bottom edge of the triangle, which we can conclude is positive since the angle is positive. Every other angle can be constructed from the original angles by conjugation by weight r

nodes, which preserves positivity and these coordinates are in Γ( J)◦. The configuration then satisfies the condition of Proposition 16.10 and so is positive as needed. □

Of course this result extends to configurations of n decorated flags: 

Theorem 16.13. A configuration in Conf × 

> n

(AΘ) is positive if and only if it is parametrized by a positive J-point for some cluster chart. In this case, it provides a positive J-point for any cluster chart. Proof. From the definition it is clear that a configuration of decorated flags is positive if and only if all the configurations of three decorated flags defined by the fan triangulation (Figure 34) are positive. Now apply Proposition 16.10 to see that a configuration is positive if and only if it is parametrized by a positive J-point for any cluster chart coming from the fan triangulation. Combining Theorem 12.3 and Lemma 6.27 we conclude that this is the case for any cluster chart. □

17. Decorated twisted local systems 

In their pioneering work, Fock and Goncharov connected cluster algebras and positivity to local systems and higher Teichmüller theory [FG06]. For split algebraic groups G and a surface 

S with boundary, they considered two moduli spaces of G-local systems with additional ‘dec-oration’ associated to the boundary components. They proved that these spaces are positive varieties and that their positive parts may be considered as analogues of Teichmüller space. We will now explain how to adapt their discussion of one of these moduli spaces to any algebraic group which admits a unique system of Jordan weights. First, let us recall the basic setup. 17.1. Marked surfaces. Let S = ¯S \ (D1 ∪ · · · ∪ Dk) be a compact, connected, oriented surface obtained by removing finitely many non-intersecting open disks from the closed surface ¯S.

Definition 17.1. A marked surface bS consists of a surface S as above, together with a set 

M = {x1, . . . , x r } ⊂ ∂S of marked points . The punctured boundary is ∂ bS := ∂S \ M .Moreover, we will always assume that ∂S ̸ = ∅. A marked surface bS of genus g admits a finite area hyperbolic structure with nonempty geodesic boundary which takes each marked point xi

to a boundary cusp if and only if (27) 4g − 4 + 2 k + r > 0 .

Alternatively, we may consider the surface S′ obtained by shrinking all unmarked boundary components to punctures. Topologically it is the same as S, but the hyperbolic structure on S′

will have a cusp at each puncture. Now, let n be the number of components of ∂ bS, and pick a set of distinguished points 

{y1, . . . , y n} =: N , one per component. Let M ′ ⊂ S′ be the set of marked points after shrink-ing unmarked holes. Similarly let N ′ be the set of distinguished points after shrinking. The distinguished points on unmarked boundary components simply become the punctures of S′.

Definition 17.2. An ideal triangulation of bS, is a maximal collection of arcs in S′ \ M ′, all of which start and end at points in N ′, considered up to homotopy with fixed endpoints. 

Remark 17.3 . When Equation (27) holds, we can also choose a finite area hyperbolic metric on 

S′ \ N ′. Then an ideal triangulation of this hyperbolic surface in the usual sense is equivalent to an ideal triangulation of the marked surface. Using this alternative approach, endow S′ \ N ′ with a hyperbolic metric. This provides us with a developing map from the universal cover eS′ to the hyperbolic plane H. The preimage of the set N ′ of distinguished points under the covering map eS′ → S′, is a set F( bS) ⊂ ∂H, called the Farey set . It is a cyclic set on which π1(S) acts. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 121 

17.2. Decorated twisted local systems. Let G be a reductive algebraic group over K and S

a surface. Recall that a G-local system on S is a flat principal G-bundle with base space S.

Remark 17.4 . The moduli space of G-local systems up to isomorphism can be identified with the space of representations π1(S) → G up to conjugation via the holonomy representation. Fock and Goncharov connected this to configurations of decorated flags by considering a slightly more complicated moduli space. We first need to handle the element sG. Instead of S,consider the punctured tangent bundle , denoted T ′S, which is obtained from the tangent bundle T S by removing the zero section. Its fundamental group provides a central extension of 

π1(S) described by the short exact sequence 

Z ι

→ π1(T ′S) → π1(S) .

The subgroup ι(Z) < π 1(T ′S) is generated by s, a loop around the fiber of T ′S → S.

Definition 17.5. A twisted G-local system on S is a G-local system on T ′S such that the holonomy around any fiber T ′S → S is sG.This is well-defined since sG is at most of order 2. 

Remark 17.6 . The moduli space of twisted G-local systems is isomorphic to the space of twisted representations up to conjugation: A twisted representation is a representation ϱ : π1(T ′S) →

G for which ϱ(s) = sG.

Remark 17.7 . If G is a group where sG = 1 , the twisting is trivial and we can safely consider local systems on S instead. Now assume that (G, Θ) is Jordan compatible. We will add some additional data to twisted local systems, which morally consists of a choice of decorated flag in AΘ at each distinguished point in N . This follows Le’s description for the split case [Le19], adapted to our setting. For a twisted G-local system L on a marked surface S, consider the associated flag bundle LA on T ′S,i.e. the associated bundle with fiber AΘ. We may lift the boundary of S to T ′S by considering the positively oriented unit tangent vector (after endowing S with an auxiliary metric). If bS

is a marked surface with underlying surface S, we may lift every component of the punctured boundary ∂ bS to T ′S in this way to obtain a set of arcs and loops, which we call the lifted boundary . Furthermore, we can restrict the flag bundle LA to these. 

Definition 17.8. A decorated twisted G-local system on a marked surface bS is a twisted local system L on S together with flat sections of LA restricted to any component of the lifted boundary. We call this choice the decoration . AG, bS denotes the moduli space of decorated twisted G-local systems on bS up to isomorphism. Suppose that bS has a boundary component with no marked point on it. The lifted boundary component represents an element of γ ∈ π1(T ′S). For any twisted G-local system L the asso-ciated holonomy ϱ(γ) ∈ G is obtained by considering a point p in the fiber of the local system over a base point x on γ. Parallel transport takes this to another point p.ϱ (γ) in the same fiber, using the right action of G. The decoration on this boundary component is a flat section of LA

along the lifted boundary γ. From the construction of the associated bundle LA , it is clear that the flat section is fully determined by its value at x which can be represented as (p, A ) for some 

A ∈ AΘ. Since it is a flat section, we can follow γ and arrive at the point  p.ϱ (γ), A  in the fiber over x, which is equivalently represented as  p, ϱ (γ)A. Since the section has to close up, the decorated flag A has to be fixed by ϱ(γ).We will now connect this to our previous discussion of configurations of decorated flags: 

Example 17.9 . Consider the marked surface bDn = ( D, {x1, . . . , x n}), i.e. a disk with n marked points on the boundary. Since π1(D) is trivial, there is a unique twisted local system L on 

D. Thus a point of AR, bDn is completely determined by the decoration. Denote by C1, . . . , C n

For a fixed triangulation T , different cluster charts arise by choosing a different vertex or different reduced expressions of w0. In this case the theorem is implied by Theorem 11.11 and Proposition 11.19. Relating cluster charts for two different triangulations can be done by realizing a sequence of flips connecting the two triangulations. This was proved in Theorem 12.3. This finishes the proof of the second statement. □

17.4. Computing holonomies. The reconstruction procedure as reviewed in the proof of The-orem 17.13 works by inscribing a graph into the surface which is determined by the ideal tri-angulation, and assigning elements in G to every (oriented) edge. Then, the holonomy of the local system can be computed by homotoping a loop in T ′S onto the graph and taking the product of group elements along it. Here is a small example, which is related to our discussion in Section 15.

Of course, this element is not a holonomy of the local system on the disk, where the only nontrivial loop is around the fiber of T ′S → S. However, since the upper and lower edge of the square correspond to the same configuration of two decorated flags, we may glue them to obtain a twisted decorated local system on the cylinder. This has nontrivial topology and the highlighted path closes up to represent the loop around the cylinder, lifted along its tangent vector to a loop in T ′S. Thus, the holonomy for this loop is g by our previous calculation. Before we discuss some further examples of how to compute holonomies, let us simplify the situation by passing from the group G to the centerless group G′ = G/Z G. A twisted G-local system L on T ′S, defines a G′-local system L′ on S. First, choose a nowhere vanishing vector field. This provides a pullback bundle on S′. Now, take the quotient by the ZG action on the fibers to obtain L′. Importantly, the isomorphism class of L′ is independent of the chosen vector field since the holonomy around fibers of L is sG, which is mapped to Id G′ . This construction induces a projection 

p : AG, bS → XG′, bS126 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD    

> (a) Contained in a cylinder. (b) Cutting off a surface without marked points.

Figure 59. Types of loops in S.of the A -space to the X -space of framed G′-local systems on S. For full details of the notion of a framed local system see [FG06]. We will only need the fact that a decoration of the twisted local system L determines a decoration of the local system L′.To compute the holonomy of the local system L′ = p(L), we consider the graph defined by the triangulation and project all the elements assigned to the edges to G′. Since the group elements for the two oriented edges of the graph along an edge of the triangulation only differ by sG, they are projected to the same element in G′, and we may collapse the edges together. After that, the holonomy is computed in the same way as before. Let us consider two types of loops in S as indicated in Figure 59. If the loop can be thickened into an annulus with one distinguished point on each boundary component, we can change the triangulation to be in the situation of Example 17.14 and can use the indicated path to compute the holonomy of the loop. Now suppose that the loop cuts off a surface which does not contain any marked points. Up to homotopy, we may assume that the boundary contains a distinguished point, so that the triangulation looks as in the pasting scheme shown in Figure 60 for a genus 2 surface. The path following the boundary, can be homotoped onto the graph coming from the triangulation as shown in the figure, which provides a way to compute its holonomy. 

Figure 60. Triangulation of a genus 2 surface with one boundary component and loop around the boundary. 17.5. LΘ-action at marked points and potentials. Given a decorated local system L we can alter its decoration at a particular marked point by rescaling the decorated flags there by an element of LΘ.Specifically let m be a marked point and l ∈ LΘ. We define (l)m · L by sending all flags A

which appear at the marked point m to Al ∗. Note that monodromy operators send A to gA 

which commutes with this scaling, so that this action is well-defined. This is an extension of the rescaling action on configurations of flags to the moduli space of decorated local systems. 

Lemma 17.15. This action is a monomial transformation of the cluster variables in every cluster chart. NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 127 

Proof. This follows from Proposition 11.10, as our cluster charts art built out of charts for triples of flags. □

We can choose a cluster chart for which the flag at the marked point m is always chosen to be opposite to the side to which the interpolating sequence is calculated. This identities a number of partial potentials δki : L → Vi which depend on the cluster chart chosen.

When we change triangulation by flipping an arc which touches m we create a singe triangle from two, see Section 16.2. In proving that this is realized by cluster mutations, we proved that the potential of the new triangle is the sum of the adjacent two potentials. Therefore the total sum is the same in all cluster charts. Since each partial potential sits inside a triple, the second statement follows from Proposi-tion 9.9. □

17.5.1. Cluster X coordinates. In noncommutative rank 0, there is a family of coordinates called 

X coordinates which are independent of the decorations of the flags. Analogous coordinates when G is Jordan split over A1 were studied in [AGRW21]. In general noncommutative rank, we propose that the set of all ratios 

Xm = {ι(δri )−1ι(δsi )}

of partial potentials in a given cluster chart associated with the same marked point m is a potential generalization. 

Lemma 17.17. The conjugacy classes of elements of Xm are independent of the rescaling action at m.Proof. We compute that ι(βi(ςi(l))( v)) = σi(βi(l)−1)ι(v)( βi(l)−1). So for any ration in Xm, (l)m·

(ι(δri )−1ι(δsi )) = βi(l)  ι(δri )−1ι(δsi ) βi(l)−1. This shows the rescaling action at m, conjugates the ratio as needed. □

We leave the noncommutative cluster structure of these ratios for future work. 17.6. Action of the cluster automorphism group. In commutative cluster algebra theory, a cluster homomorphism can be defined as a map between two cluster algebras which maps cluster variables to cluster variables. A quasi-cluster homomorphism the composition of a usual cluster homomorphism followed by a monomial transformation in the frozen cluster variables of the target algebra, see [Fra16]. A typical way to produce a quasi-cluster automorphism is to consider a sequence of cluster mutations which takes a starting seed to a new seed which has different cluster variables but with an isomorphic mutable portion of the quiver which defines it. For a split group G, Goncharov and Shen constructed a subgroup of the cluster automorphism group ΓG, bS which acts on AG, bS by [GS19]. This group contains the mapping class group of bS,the outer automorphism group Out( G), a copy of the Weyl group, W (G), for each puncture on bS, and a (possibly a quotient) of the Artin-Tits braid group of type G, Br (G), for each boundary component of bS. It is natural to conjecture that this group is in fact the entire cluster automorphism group. Now let G be Jordan split of type R and let ΓR, bS be the subgroup of cluster automorphism group associated to the split group of type R.128 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Theorem 17.18. The group ΓR, bS acts on AR, bS by noncommutative (quasi) cluster automor-phisms. 

The proof follows the split case, by ensuring that the action of each part can be realized noncommutatively as a sequence of mutations. 

The mapping class group. The mapping class group acts on a triangulation of bS by sending it to another triangulation. Any two triangulations are related by a sequence of flips and we proved in Section 12.1 that each flip is realized by a sequence of generalized cluster mutations. Furthermore the resulting seed is isomorphic (including frozen nodes/ boundary faces) and so gives an isomorphism of the cluster structure. 

Out(G). Similarly Out( R) is realized by automorphisms of the Dynkin diagram R. This acts on reduced words for w0 by permuting the letters via the automorphism of R. This action on 

AR, bS acts by changing the reduced expression for w0 in each triangle in this way. Since we can realize this change by generalized mutations, we can realize this part of the action. 

Remark 17.19 . The only noncommutative rank greater than 1 group with a nontrivial automor-phism group is type Ap.

Weyl Group action. Consider a surface, bS, with a puncture p. There is a birational action of the Weyl group, W (R), on AR, bS associated to p as follows: 

Construction 17.20. Each decorated local system L ∈ AG, bS determines a decorated flag, A,associated to the puncture p. Without loss of generality, we can assume that π(A) = PΘ. Its decoration determines total potentials wi ∈ Vi. For ςi ∈ W (R), define ςi · L by changing the decoration of A to A [ ˇβi(ι(wi))] ∗.

Lemma 17.21. The construction above is actually an action of the Weyl group. Proof. The fact that ι(a−1)( a) = a−1 implies that ςi · (ςi · L ) = L. The rest of the Weyl group relations can each be checked in the A2, B 2/C 2, G 2 cases respectively. □

Proposition 17.22. The action of W (R) is realized by generalized mutations. Proof. Consider bS a punctured disk with two marked points on its boundary and suppose that 

G is Jordan split of type A1. We consider the mutation sequence which switches the usual triangulation of this punctured bigon with a tagged triangulation. [BHR25, Example 5.15], computes the noncommutative cluster algebra in this situation, from which it is easy to see that the potential (called the total noncommutative angle there) is transformed to its inverse by this mutation sequence, which is the result of the change of decoration described above. The A1 case on any surface (with at least 2 marked points) can be reduced to this situation by moving to a triangulation which contains the desired marked point in a bigon. Now consider G Jordan split of general type. As before we can reduce to the punctured bigon case. Choose a cluster chart where the interpolating flags are placed on the sides away from the puncture. Let m be the number of instances of the reflection ςi∗ in these interpolating sequences. Consider the level i subquiver or the subnetwork between strands i and i + 1 which surrounds the puncture. This subquiver/subnetwork is exactly the one which is associated to a group of type A1 on a punctured disk with m marked points on the boundary. We use the mutation sequence applied only to this subquiver/network which changes the tagging at the central puncture. Since this only changes the cluster variables at level i, the L-distances must change as desired by the A1 calculation. □

Braid Group action. Consider a surface, bS, with a boundary component b. There is a birational action of the Artin-Tits braid group of type R, B(R), on AG, bS associated to b as follows: 

Construction 17.23. Suppose that there are d marked points on this boundary component and choose a particular one as a basepoint. A decorated local system L ∈ AG, bS determines a sequence of decorated flags {A1, A 2, . . . , A d, A d+1 = gA 1, . . . } where g ∈ G is the monodromy of L around the boundary. Write {Bi} for the corresponding sequence of undecorated flags. Assume now that NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 129

Proposition 17.24. The braid group action is realized by quasi-cluster isomorphisms. Proof. We follow the same proof as [GS19, Section 14]. The proof breaks into several cases depending on bS. In each case the mutation sequence realizing the braid symmetry is built using mutation sequences which change reduced expressions, flip edges, and the (left) reflection map, which is a monomial map. To understand the quasi-cluster nature, we first see that if all of the L-distances around the boundary we are considering are 1 then this action is a cluster automorphism on the nose. This is because the reflection map does not change any cluster variables in this case, and that the new flags A′ 

> i

from the construction are actually the flags that would be found in an interpolating sequence between Ai and Ai+1 which starts with ςi(∗) .Finally the situation when the boundary L-distances are not equal to 1 can be remedied by first rescaling the decorations so that they are 1 i.e. change A1 to A1l(A2, A 1)−1 and so on, then compute the mutation sequence, and put the scaling back. By Lemma 17.15 this amounts to a monomial transformation in the frozen variables which come from the boundary L-distances. □

17.7. Spectral description of types Ap or Cp. Goncharov and Kontsevich give a spectral description of the space of twisted decorated local systems on a surface. Each plabic network, P , encodes a topological surface called the spectral surface. 

Definition 17.25. The spectral surface ΣP associated to P is obtained as follows: we put a disk around each white and black vertices and connect them by a strip with a right-hand twist oriented from ◦ to •.The spectral surface is invariant under the local equivalence moves. 

Definition 17.26. Goncharov and Kontsevich define a twisted version of the seed group QP

(Definition 7.3) that we denote by ¯QP . The twisted seed group on a plabic graph P is the quotient of the free group generated by edges of P by the following relations: 

• The clockwise products around the the ◦ vertices is −1.

• The clockwise products around the the • vertices is (−1) v(•)−1, where v(•) is the valency of the vertex. Denote by ¯AP the noncommutative torus with group of functions given by ¯QP .The only difference between this torus and the torus AP is in the sign of the product around each vertex, which is always +1 in the case of AP .Now let S be a marked surface and fix a triangulation T . The coordinates of Goncharov and Kontsevich are given by embedding the honeycomb network Pm (Definition 7.8) in each triangle and amalgamating the twisted seed groups. 

Definition 17.27. A local system is fully twisted if the monodromy around each contractable loop is −1.

Theorem 17.28 ([GK24] Lemma 4.2,Theorem 1.6,Theorem 6.2) .

(1) Let R be a noncommutative ring. The R× valued points of ¯QP parameterize fully twisted rank 1 R-local systems on ΣP . Equivalently, ¯AP the moduli space of fully twisted rank 1 local systems on ΣP .(2) The space ¯APm is birationally equivalent to the space of configurations of complete dec-orated flags in an m-dimensional vector space over R.130 ZACHARY GREENBERG, DANI KAUFMAN, MERIK NIEMEYER, AND ANNA WIENHARD 

Figure 61. An untwisting of the honeycomb network P4 is given by multiply-ing all dashed blue edges by −1.

(3) Let Σ be a marked surface and ∆ a triangulation of Σ. We get a plabic network on Σ by inscribing Pm into each triangle of ∆, which we denote by P∆,m . Then the space ¯AP∆,m 

is birationally equivalent to the moduli space of fully twisted rank m local systems on Σ.

For G Jordan split of type Ap, we have sG = ( −1) p. The group SL m(R) is Jordan split of type Am−1 When we turn our eyes back to our coordinates, we find 

Corollary 17.29. 

(1) AP is the moduli space of rank 1 (untwisted) local systems on ΣP .(2) The space APp is birationally equivalent to the space of configurations of complete dec-orated flags in an p + 1 -dimensional vector space over R.(3) The space AP∆,p +1 is birationally equivalent to the moduli space of ( sG) twisted rank 

p + 1 local systems on Σ.

These statements seem at odds with each other when p itself is odd; how can the space of twisted and untwisted local systems on the spectral cover parameterize the same space of configurations of flags? The solution to this is that there is a (generally non-canonical) way to 

untwist a local system. One isomorphism is given by taking a point of AP∆,p +1 for p odd and multiplying some of the edges by −1 so that the products around the black and white vertices satisfy Definition 17.26. For example in Figure 61, multiplying the dashed blue edges by −1

gives an untwisting of p = 3 . There are multiply ways to pick these edges so the untwisting is not canonical. In Jordan split type Cp, our cluster charts are really charts from A2p−1 which satisfy an extra symmetry. This symmetry corresponds to a symmetry of the spectral surface, and the local systems on the spectral surface which are invariant under this symmetry parametrize the appropriate spaces of configurations decorated flags and moduli of decorated local systems. 17.8. Positivity. Now assume that G has a Θ-positive structure, i.e. each Ji is formally real. We have now endowed the subset A TG, bS ⊂ AG, bS defined by an ideal triangulation with a coordi-nate chart. We use this to define a positive structure on AG, bS :

> G, bS

.

> G, bS

is well-defined, i.e. independent of all the choices made in the construction. Proof. This is a direct consequence of Theorem 17.13 and Lemma 6.27. □

Θ-positive decorated twisted local systems may be understood in a more geometric fashion: Recall the Farey set for a surface S, F( bS) which can be realized as a subset of ∂H ∼= S1. The 2:1 cover S1 → S1 induces a double cover eF( bS) → F ( bS) with nontrivial automorphism α. eF( bS)NONCOMMUTATIVE CLUSTER VARIETIES AND MODULI SPACES OF LOCAL SYSTEMS 131 

is a cyclic set with an action of π1(T ′S), defined by s.x = α(x) where s ∈ π1(T ′S) denotes the generator of the central subgroup Z as before. 

Definition 17.32. A π1(T ′S)-equivariant configuration of flags is a map η : eF( bS) → A 

such that for each γ ∈ π1(T ′S) the configurations η and η ◦ γ agree. Formally for each γ there is a fixed gγ ∈ G such that for any x ∈ eF( bS):

η(γ.x ) = gγ .η (x)

A π1(T ′S)-equivariant configuration of flags defines a twisted representation by ϱ(γ) = gγ .

Theorem 17.33. The space AG, bS parametrizes π1(T ′S) equivariant configurations of flags. 

We call the elements ϱ and η corresponding to L ∈ AG, bS its holonomy representation and 

boundary map , respectively. The proof of this theorem is completely analogous to the split case, see [FG06]. We can use this to give a different characterization of the space A >0  

> G, bS

:

Theorem 17.34 ([FG06], Section 8.6) . The elements of A >0  

> G, bS

are those with a positive boundary map. 

Here, a map η : eF( bS) → A is called positive if any coherent lift of a cyclically ordered tuple in F( bS) to the double cover is mapped to a positive configuration of decorated flags. A coherent lift of a tuple (x1, . . . , x k) is one obtained as follows: Take the path going through the points in F( bS) in order, and lift it to a path in eF( bS) starting at a lift ex1 of x1. This passes through exactly one preimage under the projection of every point in the tuple. These define the lift. The notion of positivity is independent of the chosen coherent lift. Finally, let us consider the holonomy representation of a positive local system. Recall the projection p : AG, bS → X G′, bS . The holonomy representation ρ of the image of a decorated twisted local system under p is a map ρ : π1(S) → G′.
